and in this last case
u
is not an isolated vertex
of
G[H_{v}]
.

By (
8.4
) the case where
u
and
v
are in
A

cannot happen. Assume then that

v\in B_{1}
. By Lemma
4.6
, there exist

a,b\in H_{v}
(and hence in
A
) that are not adjacent. Assume
that
u
is also in
B_{1}
. Since
u
and
v
are adjacent, by (
8.4
) we may
assume without loss of generality that
H_{v}\subseteq H_{u}
and so

a
and
b
belong to
H_{u}
too. Then, by the definition of
K_{u}

and
K_{v}
, we get a
C_{4}
induced by
\{u^{*},v^{*},a,b\}
, a
contradiction. So
u
should be in
A,
and to avoid a
C_{4}

induced by
\{u^{*},v^{*},a,b\}
,
u^{*}
should be non-adjacent to at
least one of
a
and
b
, say
a
. In particular,
a\neq u
.
Then, by (
8.4
),
ua\notin E(G_{1})
. So
u
does
not belong to
N(H_{v})
and since
uv
is an edge of
G_{1}
, we get
that
u\in H_{v}
. Since
uv
is solid,
u
has at least one neighbor in

H_{v}
, and it is not adjacent to
a\in H_{v}
. Hence, as
H_{v}
is anticonnected, there exist non-adjacent
vertices
c,d\in H_{v}
such that
uc\notin E(G_{1})
and

ud\in E(G_{1})
. Now
u^{*}P_{u^{*}}u^{\prime*}c^{\prime}P_{c}cv^{*}du^{*}
is a hole of
length
2\ell+2
, a contradiction again.

This proves (21).

(
22)
For all
u\in V(G_{1})
and
1\leq i\leq j\leq k_{u}
,

N[u_{i}]\subseteq N[u_{j}]
.

The result follows from how vertices are ordered after the proof of
(
8.4
) (vertices in
I
), (
8.4
) (vertices in
A
or
A^{\prime}
) and
(
8.4
) (vertices in
B_{1}
or
B^{\prime}_{1}
).
This proves (22).

(
23) If
uv
is a flat edge of
G_{1}
, then
u
is
complete to
K_{v}
and
v
is complete to
K_{u}
.

By definition of a flat edge, either
u
and
v
are in
I
or one
is in
I
and the other is in
A
or in
A^{\prime}
. The result follows
from (
8.4
), (
8.4
), and
conditions (
a2
) (applied to
A
or
A^{\prime}
)
and (
i1
) of the preblowup.
This proves (23).

(
24)
If
ux
is an optional edge of
G_{1}
with
u\in A

and
x\in B_{1}
(resp.
u\in A^{\prime}
and
x\in B_{1}^{\prime}
), then
u
is
complete to
K_{x}
.

The result follows from the definition of
K_{x}
when
x\in B_{1}
.
This proves (24).

(
25)
If
ux
and
uy
are optional edges with
u\in A
,
x,y\in B_{1}

and
H_{y}\subsetneq H_{x}
(resp.
u\in A^{\prime}
,
x,y\in B_{1}^{\prime}
and

H^{\prime}_{y}\subsetneq H^{\prime}_{x}
), then every vertex of
K_{u}
with a neighbor
in
K_{y}
is complete to
K_{x}
.

Otherwise, let
u^{*}
be a vertex in
K_{u}
that has a neighbor

y^{*}
in
K_{y}
and a non-neighbor
x^{*}
in
K_{x}
. Since

H_{x}
and
H_{y}
are not disjoint,
xy
is a solid edge of
G_{1}
and
by (
8.4
),
x^{*}y^{*}\in E(G_{1})
.

Since
x
and
y
are not equivalent, there exists a vertex
a
such that
a\in N_{A}(y)\setminus N_{A}(x)
or
a\in N_{A}(x)\setminus N_{A}(y)
. In the first case, by definition of a template,
a\in A\setminus N_{A}[H_{x}]
. Then since
H_{y}\subsetneq H_{x}
and
H_{x}
is a module of
A
we get that
a
is anticomplete to
H_{x}
and hence to
H_{y}
. So
a\notin N_{A}(y)
, a contradiction; we may then conclude that
a\in N_{A}(x)\setminus N_{A}(y)

By definition of the cliques in
B
,
x^{*}a\in E(G^{*})
and
y^{*}a\notin E(G^{*})
. Therefore, to avoid a
C_{4}
induced by
\{x^{*},y^{*},u^{*},a\}
, it should be that
u^{*}a\notin E(G^{*})
.

Now
aP_{a}a^{\prime}u^{\prime*}P_{u^{*}}u^{*}y^{*}x^{*}a
is a hole of length
2\ell+2
a
contradiction.
This proves (25).

(
26)
w^{*}
(resp.
w^{\prime*}
) is a universal vertex of

G^{*}[\bigcup_{u\in A\cup B_{1}}K_{u}]
(resp.
G^{*}[\bigcup_{u\in A^{\prime}\cup B_{1}^{\prime}}K_{u}]
).

By (
5.3
),
w^{*}
is complete to
A^{*}\setminus\{w^{*}\}
and
so to
\bigcup_{u\in A}K_{u}\setminus\{w^{*}\}
. Furthermore, from the definition of

G_{1}
we know that
w^{*}
is complete to
B_{1}\setminus\{w^{*}\}
. If

w^{*}\in B_{1}
, since all edges between vertices in
B_{1}
are solid,
by (
8.4
),
w^{*}
is complete to

B^{*}\setminus\{w^{*}\}
. Similarly, if
w^{*}\in A
,
by (
8.4
) and (
8.4
), we get that

w^{*}
is complete to
B^{*}
. In both cases
w^{*}
is a universal
vertex of
G^{*}[\bigcup_{u\in A\cup B}K_{u}]
. The proof for
w^{\prime*}
is
symmetric.
This proves (26).

From all the claims above,
G^{*}
satisfies all conditions to be a
proper blowup of
G_{1}
.
∎

6
Graphs in
\mathcal{C}_{2\ell+1}
that contain a pyramid

The goal of this section is to prove the the following.

Lemma 6.1

Let
\ell\geq 3
be an integer. If
G
is a graph in

\mathcal{C}_{2\ell+1}
and
G
contains a pyramid, then one of the
following holds:

(a)

G
is a proper blowup of a twinless odd

\ell
-template;

(b)

G
has a universal vertex;

(c)

G
has a clique cutset.

The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of
Lemma
6.1
. So from here on
\ell\geq 3
is an integer and
G

is a graph in
\mathcal{C}_{2\ell+1}
that contains a pyramid
\Pi
.
By Lemma
3.10
, the three paths of
\Pi
have
length
\ell
. By Lemma
4.5
,
\Pi
is an odd

\ell
-template. Hence, we may define an integer
k
and a sequence

F_{0},F_{1},F_{2}
of induced subgraphs of
G
as follows.

•

k
is the maximum integer such that
G
contains
an odd
\ell
-template with
k
principal paths. Observe that by
Lemma
4.15
,
G
in fact contains a twinless template with

k
principal paths, because twins can be eliminated from templates
by deleting hyperedges with equal vertex-set while there are some.

•

In
G
, pick a proper blowup
F_{1}
of a twinless odd

\ell
-template
F_{0}
with
k
principal paths. Note that
F_{0}

exists and the proper
\ell
-partition needed for the proper blowup
exists by Lemma
4.16
.

•

Suppose that
F_{0}
and
F_{1}
are chosen subject to the
maximality of the vertex-set of
F_{1}
(in the sense of
inclusion). Note that possibly
F_{0}
is not a maximal template in
the sense of inclusion, it can be that a smaller template leads to a
bigger blowup (but
F_{0}
has
k
principal paths).

•

F_{2}
is obtained from
F_{1}
by adding all vertices of

G\setminus F_{1}
that are complete to
F_{1}
.

Lemma 6.2

V(F_{2})\setminus V(F_{1})
is a (possibly empty) clique that is
complete to
F_{1}
.

Proof.

Otherwise,
G
contains a
C_{4}
.
∎

We now introduce some notation. We denote by
(A,B,A^{\prime},B^{\prime},I,w,w^{\prime})
the
proper
\ell
-partition that is used to blow up
F_{0}
. When
u
is a vertex
of
F_{0}
, we denote by
K_{u}
the clique of
F_{1}
that is blown up from

u
. We set
A^{*}=\bigcup_{u\in A}K_{u}
. We use a similar notation

B^{*}
,
A^{\prime*}
,
B^{\prime*}
and
I^{*}
.

6.1
Technical lemmas

We now prove lemmas that sum up several structural properties of
G
.

Lemma 6.3

If
u\in A\cup A^{\prime}\cup I\cup\{w,w^{\prime}\}
and
v\in N_{V(F_{0})}(u)
,
then
u
is complete to
K_{v}
.

Proof.

We prove this lemma using the conditions from the definition of
blowups. If
u\in\{w,w^{\prime}\}
, then the result follows from
condition (
h
). If
u\in A\cup A^{\prime}
, then the conclusion
follows from conditions (
d
), (
e
) and (
f
). If
u\in I
, then the conclusion follows from
condition (
e
).
∎

Very often, Lemma
6.3
will be used in the
following way. Suppose there exists a principal path
P=u\dots u^{\prime}
of

F_{0}
. Suppose there exists a vertex
x
of
P
and
x^{*}\in K_{x}
.
Then by Lemma
6.3
and
condition (
c
) of blowups,

\{x^{*}\}\cup(V(P)\setminus\{x\})
induces a path of
F_{1}
. If
y\neq x

is a vertex of
P
and
y^{*}\in K_{y}
, then

\{x^{*},y^{*}\}\cup(V(P)\setminus\{x,y\})
might fail to induce a path of

F_{1}
, because it is possible that
xy\in E(G)
while

x^{*}y^{*}\notin E(G)
. But under the assumption that
x^{*}y^{*}\in E(G)

or
xy\notin E(G)
, we do have that

\{x^{*},y^{*}\}\cup(V(P)\setminus\{x,y\})
induces a path of

F_{1}
. Several variant of this situation will appear soon and we will
simply justify them by refering to
Lemma
6.3
.

When
u
is a vertex in
A
, we denote by
P_{u}
the unique principal
path of
F_{0}
that contains
u
. Its end in
A^{\prime}
is then denoted by

u^{\prime}
. We denote by
u^{+}
the neighbor of
u
in
P_{u}
. We denote by

u^{++}
the neighbor of
u^{+}
in
P_{u}\setminus u
. Note that
u^{+}\in I

and
u^{++}\in I\cup A^{\prime}
(
u^{++}\in A^{\prime}
if and only if
\ell=3
).

For any distinct
u,v\in A
, from the definition of templates,
exactly one of
V(P_{u})\cup V(P_{v})\cup\{w\}
or

V(P_{u})\cup V(P_{v})\cup\{w^{\prime}\}
induces a hole that is denoted by

C_{u,v}
. Such a hole is called a
principal hole
.

Note that there are two kinds of principal holes: those that contain

w
, and those that contain
w^{\prime}
. Recall that by
Lemma
4.11
, every hole of a template contains two
principal paths plus an extra vertex, but it may fail to be a
principal hole (because it may fail to contain
w
or
w^{\prime}
). Though
we do not use this information formally, it is worth noting that by
Lemma
6.3
, when
C
is a principal hole,

\bigcup_{v\in V(C)}K_{v}
induces a ring. But when
C
is a non-principal
hole, it may happen that
\bigcup_{v\in V(C)}K_{v}
does not induce a
ring (because there might be in
C
an optional edge
uv
with

u\in A
and
v\in B
, and after the blowup process, there might be no
vertex in
K_{v}
that is complete to
K_{u}
).

Lemma 6.4

If
u\in V(F_{0})
and
u^{*}\in K_{u}
, then
u^{*}
has two
neighbors in
V(F_{0})\setminus K_{u}
that are not adjacent.

Proof.

If
u\in I
, then let
P
be the principal path that contains
u
. By
Lemma
6.3
,
u^{*}
is adjacent to the two
neighbors of
u
in
P
.

If
u\in A\cup A^{\prime}
, say
u\in A
up to symmetry, then we claim that

u
has a neighbor
z
in
A\cup B
. This is clear if
u
is not
isolated in
A
and otherwise we set
z=w
. By
Lemma
6.3
,
z
and
u^{+}
are
non-adjacent neighbors of
u^{*}
.

If
u\in B
, then by the definition of a template,
H_{u}
contains two non adjacent vertices
a
and
b
that are neighbors of
u
.
By Lemma
6.3
,
a
and
b
are
both adjacent to
u^{*}
.
∎

Lemma 6.5

If
uv
is an edge of
F_{0}[A\cup A^{\prime}\cup I\cup\{w,w^{\prime}\}]
, then
some principal hole of
F_{0}
goes through
uv
.

Proof.

If at least one of
u
,
v
is in
I
then
uv
is an edge of a
principal path and we know that this principal path belongs to a
principal hole. Else, since
A\cup\{w\}
is
anticomplete to
A^{\prime}\cup\{w^{\prime}\}
, up to symmetry both
u
and
v
are
in
A
or
u=w\in B
and
v\in A
.

If
u,v\in A
then
C_{u,v}
is a principal hole containing
uv
.

If
u=w\in B
and
v\in A
: since
w
is in
B
,
G[A]
has no
universal vertex and there exists
a\in A
which is not adjacent to

v
. Now
w,P_{v},P_{a}
form a principal hole containing the edge

uv
.
∎

Lemma 6.6

If
K
is a clique of
F_{0}
,
K^{*}=\bigcup_{v\in K}K_{v}
and
D
is a
connected induced subgraph of
G\setminus F_{2}
such that

N_{V(F_{1})}(D)\subseteq K^{*}
, then
N_{V(F_{1})}(D)
is a clique.

Proof.

For suppose not. This means that there exists

u^{*},v^{*}\in K^{*}
and
x_{u},x_{v}\in D
such that

u^{*}v^{*}\notin E(G)
and
x_{u}u^{*},x_{v}v^{*}\in E(G)
(possibly

x_{u}=x_{v}
). Since
D
is connected, there exists a path
P
in
D

from
x_{u}
to
x_{v}
. Suppose that
u^{*}
,
x_{u}
,
v^{*}
,
x_{v}
and

P
are chosen subject to the minimality of
P
. It follows that

u^{*}x_{u}Px_{v}v^{*}
is a path, and recall that by assumption
its interior is anticomplete to
F_{1}\setminus K^{*}
.

Since
u^{*}v^{*}\notin E(G)
,
u^{*}
and
v^{*}
are in different blown-up
cliques. Denote by
K_{u}
and
K_{v}
the blown-up cliques such that

u^{*}\in K_{u}
and
v^{*}\in K_{v}
. By hypothesis,
uv\in K
and so

uv\in E(G)
. Since
u^{*}v^{*}\notin E(G)
, by
condition (
d
) of blowups,
uv
is not a solid edge of

G
.

If
uv
is a flat edge of
F_{0}
, then by Lemma
6.5
a
principal hole
C
goes through
uv
. Note that apart from
u
and

v
, no vertex of
C
is in
K
since
K
is a clique. By
Lemma
6.3
, in
G
,

(\{u^{*},v^{*}\})\cup V(C))\setminus\{u,v\}
induces a path
Q
of length

2\ell
. So
P
and
Q
form a hole of length at least
2\ell+2
,
a contradiction.

If
uv
is an optional edge of
F_{0}
, say with
u\in A
and

v\in B
, then
u\in H_{v}
, and there exists
a
in
H_{v}
such that

au\notin E(F_{0})
. Therefore,
P_{u}
,
P_{a}
and
v
form a hole

C^{*}
. By condition (
f
) of blowups (if
va
is optional),
or by condition (
d
) (if
va
is solid),
a
is complete
to
K_{v}
. By Lemma
6.3
it follows that

(\{u^{*},v^{*}\})\cup V(C^{*}))\setminus\{u,v\}
induces a path
Q
of length

2\ell
. So
P
and
Q
form a hole of length at least
2\ell+2
,
a contradiction again.
∎

When
C
is a hole of
G
, a vertex
v
of
V(G)\setminus V(C)
is

minor
w.r.t.
C
if
N_{V(C)}(v)
is included in a 3-vertex
path of
C
. A vertex of
V(G)\setminus V(C)
that is not minor w.r.t.
C
is
major

w.r.t.
C
.

Lemma 6.7

If
x\in V(G)\setminus V(F_{2})
and
C
is a principal hole of
F_{0}
, then
x

is minor w.r.t.
C
.

Proof.

Suppose up to symmetry that
w\in V(C)
and suppose
C=C_{u,v}
.
If
x
is major w.r.t.
C
, then
C
and
x
form a theta or a
wheel that is not a twin-wheel. So by Lemma
3.10
,

x
and
C
form a universal wheel. Let
P_{t}=t\dots t^{\prime}
be a
principal path where
t\neq u,v
. If
t
is complete to
\{u,v\}
,
then
xt\in E(G)
for otherwise
\{t,u,v,x\}
induces a
C_{4}
.
Hence
x
has at least 4 neighbors in
C_{u,t}
, so by
Lemma
3.10
,
x
is complete to
P_{t}
. If
t
is not
complete to
\{u,v\}
, say
tu\notin E(G)
, then
x
again has at
least 4 neighbors in
C_{u,t}
because
w\in V(C_{u,t})
, so again

x
is complete to
P_{t}
.

We proved that
x
is complete to all principal paths, so to

I\cup A\cup A^{\prime}
. Let
y\in B\cup B^{\prime}
. By definition of a
template
y
has two neighbors
a
and
b
, both in
A
or both in

A^{\prime}
, that are non-adjacent. Therefore
a
,
b
,
y
and
x
form a

C_{4}
, unless
x
is adjacent to
y
. This proves that
x
is
complete to
B\cup B^{\prime}
, and so to
V(F_{0})
.

Let
z
be a vertex of
F_{0}
and
z^{*}\in K_{z}
. By
Lemma
6.4
, there exists
a,b\in V(F_{0})
such that
z^{*}a,z^{*}b\in E(G)

and
ab\notin E(G)
, so since there is no
C_{4}
in
G
it should be that
xz^{*}\in E(G)
.
This proves that
x
is complete to
F_{1}
. Hence,
x\in V(F_{2})
, a
contradiction.
∎

Lemma 6.8

Let
a
and
b
be two non-adjacent vertices of some principal hole

C
of
F_{0}
. If some vertex
x
of
V(G)\setminus V(F_{2})
has neighbors
in both
K_{a}
and
K_{b}
, then
a
and
b
have a common neighbor
c

in
C
,
x
is adjacent to
c
, and
x
is anticomplete to every

K_{d}
such that
d\in V(C)\setminus\{a,b,c\}
.

Proof.

Let
a^{*}\in K_{a}
and
b^{*}\in K_{b}
be two neighbors of
x
.
Since
ab\notin E(G)
, by Lemma
6.3
,

\{a^{*},b^{*}\}\cup V(C)\setminus\{a,b\}
induces a hole
C^{*}
. Since

x
is adjacent to
a^{*}
and
b^{*}
, by Lemma
3.10
,
x

has another neighbor
c
in
C^{*}
(and in fact in
C
since
c\neq a^{*},b^{*}
). If
c
is not adjacent to
a^{*}
and
b^{*}
, then
x
is major
w.r.t.
C^{*}
, so by Lemma
3.10
,
C^{*}
and

x
form a universal wheel. It follows that
x
is major w.r.t.
C
, a contradiction to Lemma
6.7
.

We proved that
a
and
b
have a common neighbor
c
in
C
and that

x
is adjacent to
c
. Suppose for a contradiction that
x
has a
neighbor
d^{*}\in K_{d}
where
d\in V(C)\setminus\{a,b,c\}
. By the same
argument as above, since
x
has neighbors in
K_{d}
and
K_{c}
,
c

and
d
must have a common neighbor in
C
, and this common neighbor
must be
a
or
b
, say
a
up to symmetry. So,
x
has neighbors in

K_{d}
and
K_{b}
while
b
and
d
have no common neighbors in
C
, so
we may reach a contradiction as above.
∎

6.2
Connecting vertices of
F_{1}

We here explain how lemmas of Subsection
4.4
are
extended from
F_{0}
to
F_{1}
.

Lemma 6.9

If
u^{*}\in A^{*}\cup B^{*}
and
v^{*}\in A^{\prime*}\cup B^{\prime*}
, then there exists
in
F_{1}
a path
P^{*}
of length
\ell-1
,
\ell
or
\ell+1
from
u^{*}

to
v^{*}
that contains the interior of a principal path.

More specifically:

•

If
u^{*}\in A^{*}
and
v^{*}\in A^{\prime*}
, then
P^{*}
has length
\ell-1
or

\ell
.

•

If
u^{*}\in A^{*}
and
v^{*}\in B^{\prime*}
, or if
u^{*}\in B^{*}
and
v^{*}\in A^{\prime*}
,
then
P^{*}
has length
\ell
or
\ell+1
.

•

If
u^{*}\in B^{*}
and
v^{*}\in B^{\prime*}
, then
P^{*}
has length

\ell+1
.

Proof.

Let
u
and
v
be such that
u^{*}\in K_{u}
and
v^{*}\in K_{v}
. Let
P

be a path in
F_{0}
like in Lemma
4.13

from
u
to
v
(so
P
contains the interior of some principal path
Q
). By Lemma
6.3
,

\{u^{*},v^{*}\}\cup V(P)\setminus\{u,v\}
induces a path of the same length
as
P
that contains the interior of
Q
.
∎

Lemma 6.10

If
u^{*}\in B^{*}
and
v^{*}\in B^{\prime*}
, then there exist in
G
two paths

P^{*}
and
Q^{*}
from
u^{*}
to
v^{*}
both of length at most
\ell+1
such
that
P^{*}
(resp.
Q^{*}
) contains the interior of a principal path

P
(resp.
Q
), and
P\neq Q
.

Proof.

Let
u
and
v
be such that
u^{*}\in K_{u}
and
v^{*}\in K_{v}
. Let

P=u\dots v
and
Q=u\dots v
be as in the
conclusion of Lemma
4.12
. By
Lemma
6.3
,

\{u^{*},v^{*}\}\cup V(P)\setminus\{u,v\}
and

\{u^{*},v^{*}\}\cup V(Q)\setminus\{u,v\}
are the desired paths.
∎

Lemma 6.11

If some vertex
x
of
G
is adjacent to the ends of a path
P
of length
at most
\ell+1
of
G\setminus x
, then
x
is complete to
V(P)
.

Proof.

Otherwise, a shortest cycle in
G[V(P)\cup\{x\}]
has length at
least 4 and at most
\ell+3
. Since

\ell\geq 3
implies
\ell+3<2\ell+1
, this is a contradiction.
∎

6.3
Attaching a vertex to
F_{1}

In this subsection, we show that for all vertices
x
of
G\setminus F_{2}
,

N_{V(F_{1})}(x)
is a clique (see Lemma
6.15
). In
Figure
6
, several situations where
N_{V(F_{1})}(x)
is not
a clique are represented and we explain informally how they lead to a
contradiction. The first figure is an odd 3-template
F_{0}
with its
vertices
w
and
w^{\prime}
, and here
F_{1}=F_{0}
. Then, vertex
x_{1}
can be
included in
K_{y_{1}}
, a contradiction to the maximality of
F_{1}
(see
Lemma
6.12
). The vertex
x_{2}
cannot be included in an
existing blown up clique, but it can be added to
F_{0}
to yield a
bigger template (see Lemma
6.12
). The vertex
x_{3}
can be
added to
K_{u_{6}}
(see Lemma
6.13
). The vertex
x_{4}

can be added to
K_{u_{1}}
, but at the expense of modifying the
template (see Lemma
6.13
). The vertex
x_{5}
can be added
to
K_{i_{3}}
(see Lemma
6.14
).

The vertex
x_{6}
is kind of pathological because it cannot be
added to any blown-up clique, and does not increase the template. The idea
for this one is to observe that

\{x_{6}\}\cup V(F_{0})\setminus\{y_{1},u_{6}^{+}\}
induces a template and that
y_{1}
can be incorporated in
the set
K_{u_{6}}
and
K_{x_{6}}=K_{u_{6}^{+}}\cup\{x_{6}\}
(see
Lemma
6.15
). Note that in this case, we increase
the size of the blowup while decreasing the size of the template.

In each case, we prove that adding
x
yields a preblowup of
F_{0}
, so
that the maximality of
F_{1}
is contradicted.

Figure 6:
Vertices attaching to an odd 3-template

Lemma 6.12

If
x\in G\setminus F_{2}
has no neighbor in
I^{*}
, then
N_{V(F_{1})}(x)
is a
clique.

Proof.

Suppose for a contradiction that
N_{V(F_{1})}(x)
is not a
clique.

(
27)
We may assume that
N_{V(F_{1})}(x)\subseteq A^{*}\cup B^{*}
.

If
x
has neighbors in both
A^{*}\cup B^{*}
and
A^{\prime*}\cup B^{\prime*}
,
then consider a path
P
as in Lemma
6.9
from a
neighbor of
x
in
A^{*}\cup B^{*}
to a neighbor of
x
in

A^{\prime*}\cup B^{\prime*}
. By Lemma
6.11
,
x
is complete to

V(P)
. This is a contradiction since
x
has no neighbor in

I^{*}
. Hence
x
does not have neighbors in both
A^{*}\cup B^{*}
and

A^{\prime*}\cup B^{\prime*}
, and our claim follows up to symmetry.
This proves (27).

(
28)
There exist non-adjacent
a,b\in A
such that
x
has neighbors
in both
K_{a}
and
K_{b}
.

By Lemma
6.6
, since
N_{V(F_{1})}(x)
is not a clique,
there should exist two non-adjacent vertices
a,b\in V(F_{0})
such
that
x
has a neighbor
a^{*}\in K_{a}
and a neighbor

b^{*}\in K_{b}.
By (
9.4
),
a,b\in A\cup B.

If
a,b\in A
, then our conclusion holds, so we may assume that

b\in B
.

If
a\in A
, then since
ab\notin E(G)
,
H_{b}
is anticomplete to

a
. Let
P^{*}_{a}
be the path induced by

\{a^{*}\}\cup(V(P_{a})\setminus\{a\})
. Let
v\in H_{b}
. We may assume
that
xv\notin E(G)
for otherwise our claim holds (with
a
and

v
). Note that since
ab,av\notin E(G),
by (
c
) of blowup,
a^{*}b^{*},a^{*}v\notin E(G)
.
Now, the paths
P_{a}^{*}
,
P_{v}
,
a^{*}xb^{*}v
form a hole of
length
2\ell+2
, a contradiction. Hence, we may assume

a\in B
.

Since
ab\notin E(G)
, by Lemma
4.7
,

\{a\}\cup H_{a}
is
anticomplete to
\{b\}\cup H_{b}
. We may assume that
x
is
anticomplete to
H_{a}\cup H_{b}
for otherwise we may apply the proofs
above. Hence, for
u\in H_{a}
and
v\in H_{b}
, the
two paths
P_{u}
and
P_{v}
together with the path
ua^{*}xb^{*}v
form
a hole of length
2\ell+3
.
This proves (28).

Now the sets
K_{u}
for all
u\in A\cup A^{\prime}\cup I
,
B^{*}\cup\{x\}

and
B^{\prime*}
form a preblowup of
F_{0}
. All conditions are easily
checked. In particular
x
satisfies condition (
b
)
by (
9.4
) and (
b2
) by (
9.4
)).
So, by Lemma
5.6
,
G[V(F_{1})\cup\{x\}]
is a proper
blowup of some
\ell
-template with
k
principal paths. This contradicts
the maximality of
F_{1}
.
∎

Lemma 6.13

If there exist
x\in V(G)\setminus V(F_{2})
and
u\in A
such that
x

has neighbors in both
K_{u}
and
K_{u^{+}}
and is anticomplete to

K_{u^{++}}
, then
N_{V(F_{1})}(x)
is a clique.

Proof.

Suppose for a contradiction that
N_{V(F_{1})}(x)
is not a clique.

(
29)

x
is anticomplete to
A^{\prime*}\cup B^{\prime*}\cup(I^{*}\setminus K_{u^{+}})
.

If
x
has a neighbor
t^{*}
in some
K_{t}
such that

t\in(A^{\prime}\cup I)\setminus\{u^{+}\}
, then note that
t\neq u^{++}
by
assumption. Let
C
be a principal hole that contains
t

and
u
. There is a contradiction to Lemma
6.8
because by (
c
) of blowup

u
,
u^{+}
and
t
cannot be consecutive along
C
.

It remains to prove that
x
is anticomplete to
B^{\prime*}
. Otherwise,

x
has a neighbor
t\in B^{\prime*}
. Consider a path
P
from
t
to
the neighbor of
x
in
K_{u}
as in Lemma
6.9
and let
Q
be the principal
path whose interior is contained in
P
. By
Lemma
6.11
,
x
is complete to
V(P)
. This is a
contradiction because if
Q=P_{u}
then
x
is anticomplete to

K_{u^{++}}
, and if
Q\neq P_{u}
then we already proved that
x

is anticomplete to
(A^{\prime*}\cup I^{*})\setminus K_{u^{+}}
.
This proves (29).

From here on,
u^{*}
and
u^{+*}
are neighbors of
x
in
respectively
K_{u}
and
K_{u^{+}}
. Note that
x
has a neighbor

y^{*}\in K_{y}
for some
y\in A\cup B\setminus\{u\}
, for otherwise,
by (
9.4
),
N_{V(F_{1})}(x)\subseteq K_{u}\cup K_{u^{+}}
and
by Lemma
6.6
,
N_{V(F_{1})}(x)
is a clique, a
contradiction.

(
30)
If
w\in B
, then
x
has a neighbor
w^{*}\in B^{*}
that is
complete to
A^{*}
.

We may assume that
x
is non-adjacent to
w
, for otherwise by
condition (
h
) of blowups, we may choose
w^{*}=w
. In
particular
y^{*}\neq w
.

We claim that we may assume that
y^{*}
has a non-neighbor
v^{*}

such that
v^{*}\in K_{v}
,
v\in A
and
v\neq u
.

If
y^{*}\in B^{*}
, this is because we may assume that
y^{*}

has a non-neighbor
v^{*}\in A^{*}
(so
v^{*}\in K_{v}
for some
v\in A
)
for otherwise we choose
w^{*}=y^{*}
from the start. It remains to
check that
u\neq v
. This is because if
u=v
, then there exists
a path
Q
of length 1, 2 or 3 from
x
to
v^{*}
with interior in

K_{u^{+}}
(through
xv^{*}
,
u^{+}
,
u^{+*}
or
u^{+}u^{+*}
).
Hence,
xQv^{*}wy^{*}x
is a hole of length 4, 5 or 6, a
contradiction.

If
y^{*}\in A^{*}
, then
u^{*}y^{*}\in E(G)
for otherwise,

\{x,y^{*},w,u^{*}\}
induces a
C_{4}
. By condition (
c
)
of blowups,
uy\in E(G)
. It follows that none of
u
and
y
is
isolated in
G[A]
, so the existence of
v^{*}
follows from
Lemma
4.17
that guarantees the existence of isolated
vertices in
G[A]
since
w\in B
by assumption.

So, our claim is proved. Note that
xv^{*}\notin E(G)
for
otherwise
\{x,y^{*},w,v^{*}\}
induces a
C_{4}
. Now either

xy^{*}wv^{*}v^{+}P_{v}v^{\prime}w^{\prime}u^{\prime}P_{u}u^{++}u^{+*}x
is a hole of length

2\ell+3
(in case
u^{\prime}v^{\prime}\notin E(G)
) or

xy^{*}wv^{*}v^{+}P_{v}v^{\prime}u^{\prime}P_{u}u^{++}u^{+*}x
is a hole of length
2\ell+2

(in case
u^{\prime}v^{\prime}\in E(G)
). In both cases we get a contradiction.
This proves (30).

(
31)

N_{A}(x)\setminus\{u\}=N_{A}(u)
.

If there exists
v\in N_{A}(x)\setminus N_{A}[u]
, then

vP_{v}v^{\prime}u^{\prime}P_{u}u^{++}u^{+*}xv
is a hole of length
2\ell
, a
contradiction.

Conversely, suppose there exists
v\in N_{A}(u)\setminus N_{A}(x)
. We
claim that there exists a path
Q
of length 2 from
x
to
some
z\in N_{A}(u)
with interior in
(A^{*}\cup B^{*})\setminus(K_{u}\cup K_{z})
.

If
w\in B
, then we may choose
z=v
and
Q=xw^{*}z

by (
9.4
).

Otherwise,
w\in A
. So, by Lemma
4.17
,
G[A]

contains at least two universal vertices. So, let

t\in A\setminus\{u,v\}
be adjacent to
u
and
v
(if
u
and
v

are the universal vertices of
G[A]
,
t
can be any vertex of

A\setminus\{u,v\}
and otherwise choose
t
to be a universal
vertex).

If
x
has a neighbor
t^{*}
in
K_{t}
, then we choose
Q=xt^{*}v
.
So, suppose
x
is anticomplete to
K_{t}
(in particular,

y\neq t
). If
x
has a neighbor
v^{*}
in
K_{v}
, then we choose

Q=xv^{*}t
. So, suppose
x
is anticomplete to
K_{v}
(in
particular,
y\neq v
). Now, by the way we chose
v
and
t
, one
of
v
or
t
is a universal vertex of
G[A]
and therefore a
universal vertex of
G[A^{*}\cup B^{*}]
. So, we may choose
Q=xy^{*}v

or
Q=xy^{*}t
.

So, our claim is proved. Hence
z^{\prime}P_{z}zQxu^{+*}u^{++}P_{u}u^{\prime}w^{\prime}z^{\prime}
is
a hole of length
2\ell+2
, a contradiction.
This proves (31).

(
32)

x
is complete to
K_{u}
.

Suppose there exists
r\in K_{u}
such that
rx\notin E(G)
. We
claim that
x
and
r
have a common neighbor
z
in
(A^{*}\cup B^{*})\setminus K_{u}
.

If
w\in B
, then
rw^{*}\in E(G)
by (
9.4
) so we
may choose
z=w^{*}
. If
w\in A
, then by Lemma
4.17
,
some vertex
z\in A\setminus\{u\}
is a universal vertex of
G[A]
, and
by (
9.4
),
z
is adjacent to
x
. So,
z
exists as
claimed.

If
xu^{+}\in E(G)
then
\{r,z,u^{+},x\}
induces a
C_{4}
, a
contradiction. Hence
xu^{+}\notin E(G)
. Now by condition
(
e
) of blowups, either
\{x,z,r,u^{+*}\}
induces a

C_{4}
or
\{x,z,r,u^{+},u^{+*}\}
induces a
C_{5}
.
This proves (32).

Now, the sets
K_{v}
for all
v\in(A\setminus{u})\cup I\cup A^{\prime}
,

K_{u}\cup\{x\}
,
B^{*}
and
B^{\prime*}
form a preblowup of
F_{0}
. All
conditions are easy to check. In particular,
K_{u}\cup\{x\}
is a
clique by (
9.4
), conditions (
a
),
(
b
) and (
i
) follows from (
9.4
),
condition (
a1
) from (
9.4
), condition
(
b1
) from (
9.4
) and condition
(
a2
) from our assumptions.

Hence, by Lemma
5.6

G[V(F_{1})\cup\{x\}]
is a
proper blowup of some twinless odd
\ell
-template with
k

principal paths that is an induced subgraph of
G
a contradiction
to the maximality of
F_{1}
.
∎

Lemma 6.14

If
x\in V(G)\setminus V(F_{2})
has no neighbor in
B^{*}\cup B^{\prime*}
, then

N_{V(F_{1})}(x)
is a clique.

Proof.

Suppose for a contradiction that
N_{V(F_{1})}(x)
is not a clique. By
Lemma
6.12
,
x
has neighbors in
I^{*}
. So
x
has a
neighbor in a clique blown up from an internal vertex of some
principal path
P_{v}=v\dots v^{\prime}
. Let
a
(resp.
b
) be the
vertex of
P_{v}
closest to
v
(resp. to
v^{\prime}
) along
P_{v}
and such
that
x
has a neighbor in
K_{a}
(resp.
K_{b}
).

Suppose first that
a=b
(so
a\in I
). Then
x
has a neighbor in
some
K_{y}
with
y\in V(F_{0})\setminus\{a\}
, and since by assumption

x
has no neighbor in
B^{*}\cup B^{\prime*}
,
y\in A\cup A^{\prime}\cup I
. So,

y
and
a
are non-adjacent members of some principal hole. By
Lemma
6.8
,
x
has a neighbor in some clique
K_{d}

where
d
is adjacent to
a=b
, a contradiction to
a=b
.

Suppose now that
ab\in E(G)
. If both
a
and
b
are internal
vertices of
P_{v}
, then as in the previous paragraph, we may deduce from
Lemma
6.8
that
N_{V(F_{1})}(x)\subseteq K_{a}\cup K_{b}
. So,
by Lemma
6.6
,
N_{V(F_{1})}(x)
is a clique, a
contradiction. It follows that at least one of
a
or
b
is an
end of
P_{v}
. Up to symmetry, we may assume that
a=v
and

b=v^{+}
. Note that
x
is then anticomplete to
K_{v^{++}}
. Hence, by
Lemma
6.13
,
N_{V(F_{1})}(x)
is a clique, a contradiction.

Hence,
a\neq b
and
ab\notin E(G)
. So, by Lemma
6.8
,

a
and
b
have a common neighbor
u
in
P_{v}
. So,
a
,
u
and

b
are consecutive along
P_{v}
(in particular,
u\in I
).

(
33)

x
is complete to
K_{u}
.

Otherwise, let
u^{*}\in K_{u}
be a non-adjacent to
x
. There exists
a path
Q_{a}
of length 2 or 3 from
u^{*}
to
x
with interior in

K_{a}
(either
xa^{*}u^{*}
, or
xa^{*}au^{*}
for some
a^{*}
in
K_{a}
).
There exists a similar path
Q_{b}
. So,
Q_{a}
and
Q_{b}
form a
hole of length 4, 5 or 6, a contradiction.
This proves (33).

(
34)

x
is anticomplete to
V(F_{1})\setminus(K_{a}\cup K_{u}\cup K_{b})
.

This follows from Lemma
6.8
and from the fact that
x
is anticomplete to
B^{*}\cup B^{\prime*}
.
This proves (34).

(
35)

x
has neighbors in each of
K_{a}
,
K_{b}
.

This follows from the definition of
a
and
b
.
This proves (35).

Now the sets
K_{v}
for all
v\in(A\cup A^{\prime}\cup I)\setminus\{u\}
,
K_{u}\cup\{x\}
,

B^{*}
and
B^{\prime*}
form a preblowup of
F_{0}
. All conditions are
easily checked, in particular
K_{u}\cup\{x\}
is a clique
by (
9.4
), it satisfies condition (
i
)
by (
9.4
) and condition (
i1
)
by (
9.4
).

Hence by Lemma
5.6
,
G[V(F_{1})\cup\{x\}]
is a
proper blowup of some twinless odd
\ell
-template with
k

principal paths that is an induced subgraph of
G
. This contradicts
the maximality of
F_{1}
.
∎

Lemma 6.15

For all vertices
x
of
G\setminus F_{2}
,
N_{V(F_{1})}(x)
is a clique.

Proof.

Suppose for a contradiction that
N_{V(F_{1})}(x)
is not a clique.

(
36)
There exists a principal path
P_{u}=u\dots u^{\prime}
of
F_{0}
such that

x
is anticomplete to
I^{*}\setminus\bigcup_{v\in V(P_{u})}K_{v}
.

Otherwise, there exist two principal paths
P
and
Q
of
F_{0}
,

a
in the interior of
P
and
b
in the interior of
Q
such
that
x
has neighbors in both
K_{a}
and
K_{b}
. Note that
P
and

Q
are in some principal hole
C
of
F_{0}
. By
Lemma
6.8
,
a
and
b
have a common neighbor
c
in

C
. This contradicts
a
and
b
being in the interior of
distinct principal paths.
This proves (36).

(
37)
We may assume that
x
has no neighbor in
B^{\prime*}
and has a
neighbor
y^{*}\in K_{y}
where
y\in B
.

Suppose that
x
has a neighbor
u^{*}\in B^{*}
and a neighbor

v^{*}\in B^{\prime*}
. Let
P
and
Q
be like in
Lemma
6.10
. By Lemma
6.11
,
x
is
complete to both
V(P)
and
V(Q)
. In particular,
x
has
neighbors in the interior of two distinct principal paths, a
contradiction to (
9.4
). So, up to symmetry, we
may assume that
x
has no neighbor in
B^{\prime*}
. Hence, by
Lemma
6.14
,
x
has neighbors in
B^{*}
.
This proves (37).

(
38)

x
is adjacent to
u
,
u^{+}
and has a neighbor in

K_{u^{++}}
. Moreover,
x
is anticomplete to

(A^{*}\cup I^{*}\cup A^{\prime*}\cup B^{\prime*})\setminus(K_{u}\cup K_{u^{+}}\cup K_{u^{++}})
.

By Lemma
6.12
,
x
has at least one neighbor in
I^{*}

and by (
9.4
), such a neighbor is in a clique
blown up from an internal vertex of
P_{u}
. So, let
v
be the
vertex of
P_{u}
closest to
u^{\prime}
along
P_{u}
such that
x
has a
neighbor
v^{*}\in K_{v}
. So
v\neq u
and
v\in A^{\prime}\cup I
. We set

Q=y^{*}uP_{u}v
if
y^{*}u\in E(G)
and
Q=y^{*}wuP_{u}v
otherwise. Let

Q^{*}
be the path induced by
\{v^{*}\}\cup(V(Q)\setminus\{v\})
and
observe that
Q^{*}
has length at most
\ell+1
. By
Lemma
6.11
,
x
is complete to
Q^{*}
. If

v\notin\{u^{+},u^{++}\}
, then
x
has neighbors in at least 4
cliques blown up from vertices of
P_{u}
and this contradicts
Lemma
6.8
. If
v=u^{+}
,
x
is adjacent to
u
(since

x
is complete to
Q^{*}
) and anticomplete to
K_{u^{++}}
, so by Lemma
6.13
,

N_{V(F_{1})}(x)
is a clique, a contradiction. So,
v=u^{++}
,
meaning that
x
is adjacent to
u
and
u^{+}
, and is anticomplete
to
I^{*}\setminus(K_{u^{+}}\cup K_{u^{++}})

by (
9.4
).

If
x
has neighbors in some
K_{a}
for
a\in A\setminus\{u\}
then
x

and
C_{u,a}
contradict Lemma
6.8
. Hence
x
is
anticomplete to
A^{*}\setminus\{K_{u}\}
.

By (
9.4
),
x
is anticomplete to
B^{\prime*}
. It remains to
check that
x
is anticomplete to
A^{\prime*}\setminus K_{u^{++}}
. So,
suppose
x
has a neighbor
z^{*}
in some
K_{z}
where

z\in A^{\prime}\setminus\{u^{++}\}
. Then a principal hole that contains
z

and
u
contradicts Lemma
6.8
.
This proves (38).

Let
u^{++*}
be a neighbor of
x
in
K_{u^{++}}
and
P_{u}^{*}
be
the path induced by
(V(P_{u})\setminus\{u^{++}\})\cup\{u^{++*}\}
.

(
39)
For every
z\in B
such that
x
is adjacent to some
z^{*}
in

K_{z}
we have
N_{A}(z)=N_{A}[u]
(in particular
N_{A}(y)=N_{A}[u]
).

Suppose there exists
v\in N_{A}(z)\setminus N_{A}[u]
. By
condition (
d
) or (
f
) of blowups,

vz^{*}\in E(G)
. So, by (
9.4
),

xz^{*}vP_{v}v^{\prime}u^{\prime}P^{*}_{u}u^{++*}x
is a hole of length
2\ell
, a
contradiction. This proves that

N_{A}(z)\subseteq N_{A}[u]
. In particular,
u
has at
least one neighbor in
H_{z}
, so by condition (
e5
) of
templates,
uz\in E(G)
.

Suppose there exists
v\in N_{A}(u)\setminus N_{A}(z)
(so
z
and
v
are
not universal vertices of
G[A\cup B]
). By
condition (
c
) of blowups,
vz^{*}\notin E(G)
. By (
9.4
),
xv\notin E(G)
. Hence
xd\in E(G)

for every universal vertex
d
of
G[A\cup B]
, for otherwise

xz^{*}dvP_{v}v^{\prime}w^{\prime}u^{\prime}P_{u}^{*}u^{++*}x
is a hole of length
2\ell+2
.

Now, by (
9.4
) and Lemma
4.17
,
w\in B
. So,
there exists an isolated vertex
c\in A
. Again
by (
9.4
),
xc\notin E(G)
and
xwcP_{c}c^{\prime}u^{\prime}P_{u}^{*}u^{++*}x

is a hole of length
2\ell
, a contradiction.
This proves (39).

(
40)

N_{F_{1}}(x)\subseteq K_{u^{++}}\cup K_{u^{+}}\cup K_{u}\cup K_{y}

By (
9.4
)

N_{F_{1}}(x)\subseteq K_{u^{++}}\cup K_{u^{+}}\cup K_{u}\cup B^{*}
. Suppose there exists
z^{*}\in K_{z}
such that
xz^{*}\in E(G)

and
z\in B\setminus\{y\}
. By (
9.4
),
N_{A}(z)=N_{A}[u]
and

N_{A}(y)=N_{A}[u]
. So, by Lemma
4.15
,
y
and
z
are
twins of
F_{0}
, a contradiction.
This proves (40).

(
41)

y\neq w
.

If
y=w
, then
w\in B
. So by Lemma
4.17
, there exist
isolated vertices in
G[A]
. But by (
9.4
),

N_{A}(w)=N_{A}[u]
so
u
is a universal vertex of
G[A]
, so

G[A]
has a universal vertex and an isolated vertex, a
contradiction.
This proves (41).

(
42)

N_{K_{y}}(x)
is complete to
N_{A}[u]
.

By (
9.4
),
N_{A}(y)=N_{A}[u]
. The result follows from
conditions (
d
) and (
f
) of blowups.
This proves (42).

(
43)

x
is complete to
K_{u^{+}}
.

By (
9.4
),
ux\in E(G)
. Suppose for a contradiction
that there exists
u^{+*}\in K_{u^{+}}
non-adjacent to
x
. By
condition (
e
) of blowups,

u^{+*}u,u^{+*}u^{++}\in E(G)
. Hence
xu^{++}\notin E(G)
for
otherwise
\{x,u^{++},u^{+*},u\}
induces a
C_{4}
. But now, either

\{x,u^{++*},u^{+*},u\}
induces a
C_{4}
(if

u^{+*}u^{++*}\in E(G)
) or
\{x,u^{++*},u^{++},u^{+*},u\}

induces a
C_{5}
(if
u^{+*}u^{++*}\notin E(G)
), a contradiction.
This proves (43).

(
44)

K_{u}\cup K_{y}
is a clique.

Since by (
9.4
)
N_{A}(y)=N_{A}[u]
,
u
cannot be an
isolated vertex of
H_{y}
. Hence,
uy
is a solid edge. So, by
condition (
d
) of blowups,
K_{u}
is complete
K_{y}
.
This proves (44).

We define
B_{0}=B^{*}\setminus N_{K_{y}}(x)
.

Now the sets
K_{v}
for all
v\in(A\cup I\cup A^{\prime})\setminus\{u,u^{+}\}
,

K_{u}\cup N_{K_{y}}(x)
,
K_{u^{+}}\cup\{x\}
,
B_{0}
and
B^{\prime*}
form a
preblowup of
F_{0}
. All conditions are easy to check. In particular,

K_{u}\cup N_{K_{y}}(x)
is a clique by (
9.4
),

K_{u^{+}}\cup\{x\}
is a clique by (
9.4
),
conditions (
a
), (
b
) and (
i
) follows from
(
9.4
), condition (
a1
) from
(
9.4
), condition (
a2
) holds because
x

is complete to
N_{K_{y}}(x)
, condition (
i1
) follows from
(
9.4
) and condition (
b1
) holds because
(
9.4
) implies that if
w\in B
then
w\in B_{0}
.

Hence, by Lemma
5.6
,
G[V(F_{1})\cup\{x\}]
is a proper
blowup of some twinless odd
\ell
-template with
k
principal paths
that is an induced subgraph of
G
, a contradiction to the maximality
of
F_{1}
.
∎

6.4
Attaching a component

Lemma 6.16

If
D
is a connected component of
G\setminus F_{2}
, then
N(D)
is a
clique.

Proof.

Suppose that
N(D)
is not a clique. By Lemma
6.2
,

N_{V(F_{1})}(D)
is not a clique. So, there exist
a
and
b
in
D

such that
N_{V(F_{1})}(a)\cup N_{V(F_{1})}(b)
is not a clique, and a
path
P
from
a
to
b
in
D
. We choose
a
and
b
subject to
the minimality of the length of
P
. By
Lemma
6.15
,
a\neq b
(so
P
has length at
least 1).

We set
S^{*}_{a}=N_{V(F_{1})}(a)
and
S^{*}_{b}=N_{V(F_{1})}(b)
. By
Lemma
6.15
,
S^{*}_{a}
and
S^{*}_{b}
are both cliques.
Note that possibly
S^{*}_{a}\cap S^{*}_{b}\neq\varnothing
. We denote by

\text{int}(P)
the set of the internal vertices of
P
. We set

S^{*}_{\circ}=N_{V(F_{1})}(\text{int}(P))
.

We set
S_{a}=\{t\in V(F_{0}):S_{a}^{*}\cap K_{t}\neq\varnothing\}
. We
define
S_{b}
and
S_{\circ}
similarly. Note that
S_{a}
is possibly
not included in
S_{a}^{*}
, and the same remark holds for
S_{b}
and

S_{\circ}
.

(
45)
There exist non-adjacent
x^{*}_{a}\in S^{*}_{a}
and
x^{*}_{b}\in S^{*}_{b}
. Moreover,
for all such
x^{*}_{a}
and
x^{*}_{b}
,
x^{*}_{a}aPbx^{*}_{b}
is a path.

The existence of
x^{*}_{a}
and
x^{*}_{b}
follows from the definition of
a

and
b
, and
x^{*}_{a}aPbx^{*}_{b}
is a path because of the
minimality of
P
.
This proves (45).

(
46)

S^{*}_{a}\cup S^{*}_{\circ}
and
S^{*}_{b}\cup S^{*}_{\circ}
are cliques (in
particular,
S^{*}_{\circ}
is a (possibly empty) clique of
F_{1}
that
is complete to both
S^{*}_{a}\setminus S^{*}_{\circ}
and

S^{*}_{b}\setminus S^{*}_{\circ}
).

If
S^{*}_{a}\cup S^{*}_{\circ}
is not a clique, then let
x^{*}y^{*}
be
a non-edge in
S^{*}_{a}\cup S^{*}_{\circ}
. Since
S^{*}_{a}
is a clique
by Lemma
6.15
, we may assume

y^{*}\in S^{*}_{\circ}
. By definition of
S^{*}_{\circ}
,
y^{*}
has a
neighbor in
\text{int}(P)
, and then
x^{*},y^{*}
and some subpath of
P

contradict the minimality of
P
.

The proof is similar for
S^{*}_{b}\cup S^{*}_{\circ}
.
This proves (46).

Note that while
S^{*}_{a}\cup S^{*}_{b}
is not a
clique by assumption, it might be that
S_{a}\cup S_{b}
is a clique
(for instance when
S_{a}=\{u\}
,
S_{b}=\{v\}
and
uv
is an
optional edge of
F_{0}
).

(
47)

S_{a}\cup S_{\circ}
and
S_{b}\cup S_{\circ}
are cliques of
F_{0}
(in
particular,
S_{a}
and
S_{b}
are (non-empty) cliques of
F_{0}
and

S_{\circ}
is a (possibly empty) clique of
F_{0}
that is complete
to both
S_{a}\setminus S_{\circ}
and
S_{b}\setminus S_{\circ}
).

If
S_{a}\cup S_{\circ}
is not a clique, then let
xy
be a
non-edge of
S_{a}\cup S_{\circ}
. Since

x\in S_{a}\cup S_{\circ}
, there exists

x^{*}\in K_{x}\cap(S^{*}_{a}\cup S^{*}_{\circ})
and

y^{*}\in K_{y}\cap(S^{*}_{a}\cup S^{*}_{\circ})
. By
condition (
c
) of blowups, since
xy\notin E(G)
,

K_{x}
is anticomplete to
K_{y}
. So,
x^{*}y^{*}\notin E(G)
, a
contradiction to (
6.4
).

The proof is similar for
S_{b}\cup S_{\circ}
.
This proves (47).

(
48)
If a hole
C
of
F_{1}
contains two non adjacent vertices

x\in S^{*}_{a}
and
y\in S^{*}_{b}
, then
P
and
C
form a pyramid

\Pi_{C,x,y}
. More specifically,
C
contains a vertex
z
such
that either:

•

S^{*}_{a}\cap V(C)=\{x,z\}
,
S^{*}_{b}\cap V(C)=\{y\}
; the
apex of
\Pi_{C,x,y}
is
y
, its triangle is
axz
, and its
three paths, all of length
\ell
, are the path from
x
to
y
in

C\setminus z
, the path from
y
to
z
in
C\setminus x
, and the path
from
a
to
y
obtained by adding the edge
by
to
P
; or

•

S^{*}_{b}\cap V(C)=\{y,z\}
,
S^{*}_{a}\cap V(C)=\{x\}
; the
apex of
\Pi_{C,x,y}
is
x
, its triangle is
byz
, and its
three paths, all of length
\ell
, are the path between
y
and

x
in
C\setminus z
, the path from
z
to
x
in
C\setminus y
, and
the path from
b
to
x
obtained by adding the edge
ax
to

P
.

Note that since
S^{*}_{a}
is a clique,

S^{*}_{a}\cap V(C)
contains
x
and at most one other vertex which
should be adjacent to
x
. The same holds for
S^{*}_{b}
and
y
.

Let us assume that
S^{*}_{\circ}\cap V(C)\neq\varnothing
. Then
by (
6.4
), there exists a unique
vertex
t\in S^{*}_{\circ}\cap V(C)
,

S^{*}_{a}\cap V(C)\subseteq\{x,t\}
and

S^{*}_{b}\cap V(C)\subseteq\{y,t\}
. Hence
C
and
P
form a
proper wheel centered at
t
, a contradiction to
Lemma
3.10
. So,

S^{*}_{\circ}\cap V(C)=\varnothing
.

If
a
and
b
have a common neighbor
t
in
C
, then
x
and
y

are the two neighbors of
t
in
C
and so,
C
and
P
form a
proper wheel centered at
t
, again a contradiction to
Lemma
3.10
. So the neighborhoods of
a
and
b

in
C
are disjoint.

From this, we obtain that
C
and
P
form a theta, a prism or a
pyramid. So, by Lemma
3.10
,
C
and
P
form a
pyramid whose three paths have length
\ell
. This can happen only
if we are in one of the two cases described in (
6.4
).
This proves (48).

(
49)

S_{a}\cap I=S_{b}\cap I=\varnothing
.

Otherwise, up to symmetry,
S_{a}\cap I\neq\varnothing
. So, there
exists a principal path
P_{u}=u\dots u^{\prime}
of
F_{0}
whose interior
intersects
S_{a}
. By (
6.4
),
S_{a}
is a clique, so

1\leq|S_{a}|\leq 2
and
S_{a}\subseteq V(P_{u})
.
We now break into three cases.

Case 1:

S_{b}\subseteq V(P_{u})
.

By (
6.4
) there exist vertices
x_{a}
and

x_{b}
of
P_{u}
such that there exist non adjacent vertices
x^{*}_{a}\in S^{*}_{a}\cap K_{x_{a}}
and
x^{*}_{b}\in S^{*}_{b}\cap K_{x_{b}}
.

We first show that there exist such
x_{a}
and
x_{b}
that are not adjacent. Otherwise, and since
S_{a},S_{b}\subseteq V(P_{u})
, we have that
S_{a}\cup S_{b}=\{x_{a},x_{b}\}
. By replacing
x_{a}
and
x_{b}
by
x^{*}_{a}
and
x^{*}_{b}
in any principal hole
C
containing
P_{u}
we obtain a path
P_{C}
of length
2\ell
and
V(P_{C})\cup V(P)
induces a hole
of length at least
2\ell+3
, a contradiction. So we may assume that
x_{a}
and
x_{b}
are not adjacent.

Let
C
be any principal hole of
F_{0}
that contains

P_{u}
. By Lemma
6.3
,

\{x^{*}_{a},x^{*}_{b}\}\cup(V(C)\setminus\{x_{a},x_{b}\})
induces a hole

C^{*}
. Let us apply (
6.4
) to
C^{*}
,
x^{*}_{a}
and

x^{*}_{b}
. We obtain that the shortest path in
C^{*}
between
x^{*}_{a}

and
x^{*}_{b}
has length
\ell
. However
x^{*}_{a}
and
x^{*}_{b}
both
belong to the path of length
\ell-1
, contained in
C^{*}
, which is
obtained from
P_{u}
by replacing
x_{a}
by
x^{*}_{a}
and
x_{b}
by

x^{*}_{b}
, a contradiction.

Case 2:

S_{b}
contains a vertex of some principal
path
P_{v}
distinct from
P_{u}
. Up to symmetry, since
S_{b}
is a
clique (by (
6.4
)), we assume that
b
is anticomplete to
K_{v^{\prime}}
.

Let
y
be the vertex of
P_{u}
closest to
u^{\prime}
such that
a
has a
neighbor
y^{*}\in K_{y}
. Let
z
be the vertex of
P_{v}
closest to

v
such that
b
has a neighbor
z^{*}\in K_{z}
. Possibly
y=u^{\prime}

and
z=v
, but
y\neq u
since
a
has a neighbor in

I^{*}
by assumption, and
z\neq v^{\prime}
since
b
is anticomplete to
K_{v^{\prime}}
. In particular,
yz\notin E(G)
and by
condition (
c
) of blowups,
y^{*}z^{*}\notin E(G)
.

Let
C
be the principal hole of
F_{0}
that contains
P_{u}
and

P_{v}
. By Lemma
6.3
,

\{y^{*},z^{*}\}\cup(V(C)\setminus\{y,z\})
induces a hole
C^{*}
.
Applying (
6.4
) to
C^{*}
,
y^{*}
and
z^{*}
, we obtain that

P
has length
\ell-1
and that
y^{*}
and
z^{*}
are at distance
\ell
on
C^{*}
. Hence
y^{*}
and
z^{*}
have no common neighbor in
F_{0}
and
S_{\circ}=\varnothing
by (
6.4
).
We denote by
P^{*}_{u}
the path
obtained from
P_{u}
by replacing
y
by
y^{*}
and by
P^{*}_{v}
the
path obtained from
P_{v}
by replacing
z
by
z^{*}
. Let
P^{*}
be
the path
vP^{*}_{v}z^{*}bPay^{*}P^{*}_{u}u^{\prime}
(in case
z=v
one should replace
vP^{*}_{v}z^{*}
by
z^{*}
, and in case
y=u^{\prime}
one should replace
y^{*}P^{*}_{u}u^{\prime}
by
y^{*}
). The length of
P^{*}
is at least

\ell+1
.

Consider now any principal path
P_{r}
for
r\in A\setminus\{u,v\}
.
Depending on the adjacencies of
r
with
u
and
v
, one of

rvP^{*}u^{\prime}r^{\prime}P_{r}r
or
rwvP^{*}u^{\prime}r^{\prime}P_{r}r
or
rvP^{*}u^{\prime}w^{\prime}r^{\prime}P_{r}r
or

rwvP^{*}u^{\prime}w^{\prime}r^{\prime}P_{r}r
(with possibly
u^{\prime}
replaced by
y^{*}
when

u^{\prime}=y
) is a cycle of length at least
2\ell+2
with at most one
chord that must be
br
(observe that
ar^{\prime}
cannot be an edge since
S_{a}\subseteq V(P_{u})
). The only possibility which avoids a hole
of forbidden length is if
z=v
,
y=u^{\prime}
and
br,u^{\prime}r^{\prime},vr
are edges
of
G
. This proves that
v
is complete to
A\setminus\{u,v\}
and

u^{\prime}
is complete to
A^{\prime}\setminus\{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\}
.

Hence,
G[A]
has at most one isolated vertex (namely
u
), and

G[A^{\prime}]
has at most one isolated vertex (namely
v^{\prime}
). This
contradicts
(A,B,A^{\prime},B^{\prime},I,w,w^{\prime})
being a proper

\ell
-partition of
F_{0}
.

Case 3:
we are neither in Case 1 nor in Case 2.

Since we are not in Case 1,
S_{b}
contains a vertex of
F_{0}\setminus P_{u}
,
and since we are not in Case 2, this vertex must be in
B\cup B^{\prime}
.
Up to symmetry, we assume that
S_{b}\cap B\neq\varnothing
. Since

S_{b}
is a clique (by (
6.4
)),
S_{b}\cap(B^{\prime}\cup A^{\prime}\cup I)=\varnothing
. Since
we are not in Case 2,
S_{b}\cap(A\setminus\{u\})=\varnothing
.
Hence,
S_{b}\subseteq B\cup\{u\}
and there exists
x\in B\cap S_{b}
. Let
x^{*}\in K_{x}\cap S^{*}_{b}
.

Let
u_{a}
be the vertex of
S_{a}
which is the closest to
u
in

P_{u}
and let
u^{\prime}_{a}
be the vertex of
S_{a}
which is the closest to

u^{\prime}
in
P_{u}
. Notice that, since
S_{a}
is a clique (by (
6.4
)), either
u_{a}=u^{\prime}_{a}
or
u_{a}u^{\prime}_{a}
is an edge. So it may be that
u_{a}=u
or
u^{\prime}_{a}=u^{\prime}
but since
S_{a}\cap I\neq\varnothing
we know that
u_{a}\neq u^{\prime}
and
u^{\prime}_{a}\neq u
. Let now

u^{*}_{a}\in K_{u_{a}}\cap S^{*}_{a}
and
u^{\prime*}_{a}\in K_{u^{\prime}_{a}}\cap S^{*}_{a}
. We denote by
P^{*}_{u}
the path obtained from
P_{u}
by replacing
u_{a}
by
u^{*}_{a}

and, in case
u_{a}\neq u^{\prime}_{a}
, by replacing
u^{\prime}_{a}
by
u^{\prime*}_{a}
.
Notice that if
u^{*}_{a}\neq u^{\prime*}_{a}
then

u^{*}_{a}u^{\prime*}_{a}\in E(G)
since
S_{a}^{*}
is a clique.

Suppose that
u^{\prime}_{a}=u^{+}
, where
u^{+}
is the neighbor of
u
in
P_{u}
. Since
H_{x}
contains at least two vertices there exists
v\in H_{x}\setminus\{u\}
. By (
6.4
) and the fact that
P
contains at least one edge, depending on the adjacency of
u
and
v
, one of
aPbx^{*}vP_{v}v^{\prime}u^{\prime}P^{*}_{u}u^{\prime*}_{a}a
or
aPbx^{*}vP_{v}v^{\prime}w^{\prime}u^{\prime}P^{*}_{u}u^{\prime*}_{a}a
is a hole of length at least
2\ell+2
, a contradiction. Hence from now on, we may assume that
u_{a}\neq u
(hence
a
is not adjacent to
u
) and that if
u_{a}=u^{+}
then
u^{\prime}_{a}\neq u_{a}
. Now by (
6.4
) we get that
S_{\circ}=\varnothing
.

Suppose that
x
is adjacent to
u
in
F_{0}
. Depending on whether
b
is
adjacent to
u
not, one of
u^{*}_{a}aPbuP^{*}_{u}u^{*}_{a}
or

u^{*}_{a}aPbx^{*}uP^{*}_{u}u^{*}_{a}
is a hole, implying that
P
has length at
least
\ell
.
Let us choose any vertex
v\in H_{x}
distinct from
u
(since
H_{x}
has cardinality at least 2, such a vertex do exist).
Then
aPbx^{*}vP_{v}v^{\prime}(w^{\prime})u^{\prime}P_{u}u^{\prime*}_{a}a
is a hole of length at least
2\ell+2
, a contradiction. Hence, from here on, we may assume that no vertex in

B\cap S_{b}
is adjacent to
u
.

So
x
is not adjacent to
u
in
F_{0}
. Hence
x^{*}\neq w
,
u\neq w
and
w\notin S_{b}
. Then to avoid a
C_{4}

bx^{*}wub
,
b
is not adjacent to
u

and
u^{*}_{a}aPbx^{*}wuP^{*}_{u}u^{*}_{a}
is a hole implying that
P
has length at
least
\ell-1
. So, for any
v\in H_{x}
, the hole

x^{*}bPau^{\prime*}_{a}P^{*}_{u}u^{\prime}v^{\prime}P_{v}vx^{*}
(in case
u^{\prime}_{a}=u^{\prime}
one should
replace
u^{\prime*}_{a}P^{*}u^{\prime}
by
u^{\prime*}_{a}
) has length at least
2\ell+2
,
a contradiction.

This proves (49).

(
50)
We may assume that
S_{a}\subseteq A\cup B
and

S_{b}\subseteq A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime}
.

Otherwise, by (
6.4
) and since
S_{a}
and
S_{b}
are cliques (by (
6.4
)),
we may assume that
S_{a},S_{b}\subseteq A\cup B
.

We claim that there exist non-adjacent vertices
x^{*}\in S^{*}_{a}
and

y^{*}\in S^{*}_{b}
, and a path
Q^{*}
from
x^{*}
to
y^{*}
of length at
least
2\ell-1
that forms a hole together with
P
. This is a
contradiction because it implies that
P
has length at most
0
.
So, to conclude the proof, it remains to prove the existence of

Q^{*}
.

By (
6.4
), there exist non-adjacent
x^{*}_{a}\in S^{*}_{a}
and

x^{*}_{b}\in S^{*}_{b}
. Let
x_{a}
and
x_{b}
be the vertices of
F_{0}
such
that
x^{*}_{a}\in K_{x_{a}}
and
x^{*}_{b}\in K_{x_{b}}
. Note that
possibly
x_{a}x_{b}
is an edge, but this happens only if
x_{a}x_{b}
is
an optional edge of
F_{0}
(since
x^{*}_{a}x^{*}_{b}
is not an edge). We break into three cases.

Case 1:

x_{a},x_{b}\in A.

Then
x_{a}x_{b}\notin E(G)
(otherwise it would be a solid edge of

F_{0}
), so from the definition of templates, there exists a path
Q

of length
2\ell-1
from
x_{a}
to
x_{b}
whose interior is in

I\cup A^{\prime}
. By Lemma
6.3
,

\{x^{*}_{a},x^{*}_{b}\}\cup(V(Q)\setminus\{x_{a},x_{b}\})
induces the path

Q^{*}
that we are looking for. Note that
Q^{*}
and
P
form a hole
by (
6.4
), our assumption that

S_{a},S_{b}\subseteq A\cup B
, and (
6.4
).

Case 2:

x_{a}\in A
and
x_{b}\in B.

Whether
x_{a}x_{b}
is an optional edge or a non-edge, an immediate
consequence of the definition of a template is that there exists a
vertex
z\in H_{x_{b}}
that is non-adjacent to
x_{a}
. We may
furthermore assume that
z\notin S_{b}
since else we are in the same
situation as in Case 1. By definition of a template, there exists a
path
Q_{0}
of length
2\ell-1
between
x
and
z
whose interior is
in
I\cup A^{\prime}
. Then
x_{b}zQ_{0}x_{a}
is a path of length

2\ell
and by replacing in this path
x_{a}
and
x_{b}
by respectively

x^{*}_{a}
and
x^{*}_{b},
we obtain by
Lemma
6.3
a path
Q^{*}
of the same
length. Note that
Q^{*}
and
P
form a hole
by (
6.4
), (
6.4
), our assumption that

S_{a},S_{b}\subseteq A\cup B
,
z\notin S_{b}
and
z\notin S_{a}
(since

S_{a}
is a clique).

Case 3:

x_{a},x_{b}\in B.

Then
x_{a}x_{b}\notin E(G)
(otherwise it would be a solid edge of

F_{0}
). Hence, by Lemma
4.7
,

H_{x_{a}}\cup\{x_{a}\}
is anticomplete to
H_{x_{b}}\cup\{x_{b}\}
. So,
let
u_{a}\in H_{x_{a}}
and
u_{b}\in H_{x_{b}}
, there exists then a path

Q_{0}=u_{a}\dots u_{b}
of length
2\ell-1
with interior in

I\cup A^{\prime}
. By Lemma
6.3
,

Q^{*}=x^{*}_{a}u_{a}Q_{0}u_{b}x^{*}_{b}
is also a path, it is of length
2\ell+1
.
We may assume that
u_{a}\notin S_{a}
and
u_{b}\notin S_{b}
since else we
are in the same situation as in Case 2. Now,
by (
6.4
) and (
6.4
),
Q^{*}
and
P
form a hole of
length at least
2\ell+4
.

This proves (50).

(
51)

S_{\circ}=\varnothing
.

By (
6.4
) and (
6.4
), if

S_{\circ}\neq\varnothing
, then
\ell=3
, and there exists a unique principal
path
P_{u}=u\dots u^{\prime}
of
F_{0}
such that
S_{a}=\{u\},

S_{b}=\{u^{\prime}\}

and
S_{\circ}=\{c\}
where
c
is the unique internal vertex of

P_{u}
. Let
u^{*}\in K_{u}\cap S^{*}_{a}
,
c^{*}\in S^{*}_{\circ}
and

u^{\prime*}\in K_{u^{\prime}}\cap S^{*}_{b}
. Observe that by (
6.4
)

c^{*}u^{*},c^{*}u^{\prime*}\in E(G)
.

Let
P_{v}=v\dots v^{\prime}
be a principal path distinct from
P_{u}
and
suppose up to symmetry that
uv\in E(G)
. Now by (
6.4
),
P_{v}
,
P
,
u^{*}
,

u^{\prime*}
,
w^{\prime}
and
c^{*}
form a proper wheel centered at
c^{*}
, a
contradiction to Lemma
3.10
.
This proves (51).

(
52)

P
has length
\ell-1
, or P has length
\ell-2
and we may assume
that
S_{a}\cap A=\varnothing
.

By (
6.4
) and (
6.4
), consider
non-adjacent
x^{*}\in S^{*}_{a}
and
y^{*}\in S^{*}_{b}
where
x^{*}\in K_{x}\cap S^{*}_{a}
and
y^{*}\in K_{y}\cap S^{*}_{b}
for
x\in S_{a}\cap(A\cup B)
and
y\in S_{b}\cap(A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime})
.

If
x\in A
and
y\in A^{\prime}
, then let
C
be a principal hole that
contains
x
and
y
. By Lemma
6.3
,

\{x^{*},y^{*}\}\cup(V(P)\setminus\{x,y\})
induces a hole
C^{*}
. We may
apply (
6.4
) to
C^{*}
,
x^{*}
and
y^{*}
. It follows that

P
has length
\ell-1
. By symmetry we may therefore assume from
here on that
S_{a}\cap A=\varnothing
.

Let
y^{\prime*}
be a vertex in
S^{*}_{b}
which is the closest to
x^{*}
in
F_{1}
. By Lemma
6.9
, there exists a path
Q
in
F_{1}

from
x^{*}
to
y^{\prime*}
of length
\ell
or
\ell+1
. From our assumption on
y^{\prime*}
we get that
Q
and
P

form a hole (since
S_{\circ}=\varnothing

by (
6.4
)). Therefore, if
Q
has length
\ell
, then

P
has length
\ell-1
and if
Q
has length
\ell+1
, then
P
has
length
\ell-2
.
This proves (52).

We may now conclude the proof.

If
P
has length
\ell-1
, then we set
A_{0}=A\cup\{a\}
,

A^{\prime}_{0}=A^{\prime}\cup\{b\}
and
I_{0}=I\cup\text{int}(P)
. We claim that

(A_{0},B,A^{\prime}_{0},B^{\prime},I_{0})
is an
\ell
-pretemplate partition of

G[A_{0}\cup B\cup A^{\prime}_{0}\cup B^{\prime}\cup I_{0}]
. All conditions are easily
checked to hold (in particular conditions (
a
), (
b
)
and (
g
) are satisfied because
by (
6.4
),
a
(resp.
b
) has a
neighbor in
G[A\cup B]
(resp.
G[A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime}]
), condition (
e
) holds by (
6.4
) and
conditions (
h
) and (
i
) hold because they hold in

F_{0}
). Then, by Lemma
4.14
,
G
contains an odd

\ell
-template with
k+1
principal paths, a contradiction to the
maximality of
k
.

So, by (
6.4
),
P
has length
\ell-2
and we may assume that

S_{a}\cap A=\varnothing
and
S_{a}\cap B\neq\varnothing
(recall that
by (
6.4
),
S_{a}\subseteq A\cup B
and

S_{b}\subseteq A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime}
). Let us choose
x\in S_{a}\cap B
such
that
H_{x}
is maximal (note that
x
is unique because
S_{a}\cap B
is
a clique and
F_{0}
is twinless). Let
x^{*}\in K_{x}\cap S^{*}_{a}
. We set

A_{0}=A\cup\{x^{*}\}
,
B_{0}=B\setminus S_{a}
,
A^{\prime}_{0}=A^{\prime}\cup\{b\}
and

I_{0}=I\cup\text{int}(P)\cup\{a\}
. Note that the path
x^{*}aPb

has length
\ell-1
and has interior in
I_{0}
.
We break into two cases.

Case 1:

b
has a neighbor in
A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime*}
.

We claim that in that case

(A_{0},B_{0},A^{\prime}_{0},B^{\prime*},I_{0})
is an
\ell
-pretemplate partition of

G[A_{0}\cup B_{0}\cup A^{\prime}_{0}\cup B^{\prime*}\cup I_{0}]
. All conditions are
easily checked to hold (in particular condition (
g
) is
satisfied for
A_{0}\cup B_{0}
because if
x=w
, then
x^{*}
is complete
to
(A_{0}\cup B_{0})\setminus\{x^{*}\}
, and otherwise, by the maximality of

H_{x}
,
w\in A_{0}\cup B_{0},
condition (
g
) is satisfied for

A^{\prime}_{0}\cup B^{\prime*}
because
b
has a neighbor in
A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime*}
and by the rules of the blowup,
conditions (
a
), (
b
), (
h
) and (
i
) hold because they hold in

F_{0}
and by the rules of the blowup). Then, by Lemma
4.14
,
G
contains an odd

\ell
-template in with
k+1
principal paths, a contradiction to the
maximality of
k
.

Case 2:

b
has no neighbor in
A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime*}
.

Then, by (
6.4
) there exists
x^{\prime*}\in K_{x^{\prime}}\cap S^{*}_{b}
for some
x^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}
.

Let
A^{\prime}_{1}=(A^{\prime}_{0}\cup\{x^{\prime*}\})\setminus\{x^{\prime}\}
. If
w^{\prime}\in B^{\prime}
we set
B^{\prime}_{1}=\{w^{\prime}\}
and else we set
B^{\prime}_{1}=\varnothing
.
We claim that
(A_{0},B_{0},A^{\prime}_{1},B^{\prime}_{1},I_{0})
is an
\ell
-pretemplate partition of

G[A_{0}\cup B_{0}\cup A^{\prime}_{1}\cup B^{\prime}_{1}\cup I_{0}]
.

Most conditions are easily checked to hold as in the previous case. Notice that conditions (
g
) and (
i
) hold because
x^{\prime*}
is by definition adjacent to
b
and by the rules of the blowup,
G[A^{\prime}_{1}\setminus\{b\}]
is isomorphic to
G[A^{\prime}_{0}\setminus\{b\}]
and
x^{\prime*}
is adjacent to
w^{\prime}
.
Then, by Lemma
4.14
,
G
contains an odd

\ell
-template in with
k+1
principal paths, a contradiction to the
maximality of
k
.
∎

6.5
End of the proof

We may now conclude the proof of Lemma
6.1
.
If
G\setminus F_{1}
is empty, then conclusion (
a
)
holds. If
G\setminus F_{1}
is non-empty and
G\setminus F_{2}
is empty, then
conclusion (
b
) holds. Otherwise, we consider a connected
component
D
of
G\setminus F_{2}
and apply Lemma
6.16
. We
then see that
G
has a clique cutset, so
conclusion (
c
) holds.

7
Proof of Theorem
7.1

Theorem 7.1

Let
\ell\geq 3
be an integer. If
G
is a graph in
\mathcal{C}_{2\ell+1}
then
one of the following holds:

(a)

G
is a ring of length
2\ell+1
;

(b)

G
is a proper blowup of a twinless odd

\ell
-template;

(c)

G
has a universal vertex or

(d)

G
has a clique cutset.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.10
,
G
contains no prism no theta and no
proper wheel. Also, clearly
G
contains no
C_{4}
and no
C_{5}
.
Hence, by Theorem
3.13
, we may assume that
G
contains a
pyramid for otherwise one of the conclusions (
a
),
(
c
) or (
d
) holds. The result then follows
from Lemma
6.1
.
∎

8
Even templates

8.1
Even
\ell
-template partitions

For an integer
\ell\geq 4
, an
even
\ell
-template partition
of a graph
G
is a
partition of the vertex-set of
G
into five sets
A
,
B
,

A^{\prime}
,
B^{\prime}
and
I
satisfying the following conditions.

(a)

A=A_{K}\cup A_{S}
where

A_{K}=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\}
,
A_{S}=\{v_{k+1},\ldots,v_{k+s}\}
and
k+s\geq 3
.

(b)

A^{\prime}=A^{\prime}_{K}\cup A^{\prime}_{S}
where

A^{\prime}_{K}=\{v^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,v^{\prime}_{k}\}
and
A^{\prime}_{S}=\{v^{\prime}_{k+1},\ldots,v^{\prime}_{k+s}\}
.

(c)

For each
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
,
v_{i}
and
v^{\prime}_{i}
are linked by a path of
G
of
length
\ell-1
and for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,s\}
,
v_{k+i}
and
v^{\prime}_{k+i}
are linked by a path of
G
of
length
\ell-2
. These
k+s
paths are vertex
disjoint and they are called the
principal paths
of the partition.

(d)

I
is the set of all internal vertices of the principal paths, every vertex in
I
has degree
2
in
G
.

(e)

Both
A_{K}
and
A^{\prime}_{K}
are cliques of
G
and both
A_{S}
and
A^{\prime}_{S}
are stable sets of
G
. For
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
and
j\in\{1,\ldots,s\}
, exactly one of
v_{i}v_{k+j}
and
v^{\prime}_{i}v^{\prime}_{k+j}
is an edge. Furthermore
G[A]
and hence
G[A^{\prime}]
are threshold graphs.

(f)

There exists a laminar hypergraph
\mathcal{H}
on vertex set

\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k+s}\}
such that:

-

every hyperedge
X
of
\mathcal{H}
is an anticonnected
module of
G[A]
of cardinality at least
2
and

-

if
G[A]
is not connected then at least one hyperedge of
\mathcal{H}
contains all vertices of
A
.

(g)

There exists a laminar hypergraph
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
on vertex set

\{v^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,v^{\prime}_{k+s}\}
such that:

-

every hyperedge
X^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
is a
module of
G[A^{\prime}]
of cardinality at least
2
and

-

if
G[A^{\prime}]
is not connected then least one hyperedge of
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
contains all vertices of
A^{\prime}
.

(h)

B=\{v_{X}:X\text{ hyperedge of }\mathcal{H}\}
,
B^{\prime}=\{v^{\prime}_{X}:X\text{ hyperedge of }\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\}
.

The set of edges of
G
incident to vertices in
B\cup B^{\prime}
is defined as follows:

(i)

for every
v_{X},v_{Y}\in B
,
v_{X}v_{Y}\in E(G)
if and only if
X\cap Y\neq\varnothing
,

(j)

for every
v^{\prime}_{X},v^{\prime}_{Y}\in B^{\prime}
,
v^{\prime}_{X}v^{\prime}_{Y}\in E(G)
if and only if
X\cap Y\neq\varnothing
,

(k)

for every
v_{i}\in A
,
v_{X}\in B
,
v_{i}v_{X}\in E(G)
if and only if
v_{i}\in N_{G[A]}[X]
,

(l)

for every
v^{\prime}_{i}\in A^{\prime}
,
v^{\prime}_{X}\in B^{\prime}
,
v^{\prime}_{i}v^{\prime}_{X}\in E(G)
if and only if
v^{\prime}_{i}\in N_{G[A^{\prime}]}[X]
.

The following notation is convenient.

Notation:
For every vertex
x\in B
such that
x=v_{X}

where
X
is a hyperedge of
\mathcal{H}
, we set

H_{x}=X
. Similarly, for every vertex
x\in B^{\prime}
such
that
x=v^{\prime}_{X}
where
X
is a hyperedge of
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
, we set

H^{\prime}_{x}=X
.

We now list some properties of even
\ell
-template partitions that follow directly from the definition.

(i)

G[A]

and
G[A^{\prime}]
are threshold graphs such that, -
A=A_{K}\cup A_{S}
and
A^{\prime}=A^{\prime}_{K}\cup A^{\prime}_{S}
, -
G[A_{K}]
and
G[A^{\prime}_{K}]
are complete graphs having the same number of vertices, -
G[A_{S}]
and
G[A^{\prime}_{S}]
are complement of complete graphs having the same number of vertices, - the subgraph of
G[A]
induced by the edges between
A_{K}
and
A_{S}
is isomorphic to the complement of the subgraph of
G[A^{\prime}]
induced by the edges between
A^{\prime}_{K}
and
A^{\prime}_{S}
.

(ii)

For all
x\in B
,
H_{x}
is a
module of
G[A]
and
G[H_{x}]
is anticonnected. Also for all

x\in B^{\prime}
,
H^{\prime}_{x}
is a module of
G[A^{\prime}]
and
G[H^{\prime}_{x}]
is
anticonnected.

(iii)

G[B]
is isomorphic to the line graph of the hypergraph

\mathcal{H}
on vertex set
A
and hyperedge set

\{H_{x}:x\in B\}
. Also
G[B^{\prime}]
is isomorphic to the line graph of
the hypergraph
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
on vertex set
A^{\prime}
and hyperedge
set
\{H^{\prime}_{x}:x\in B^{\prime}\}
. Hence
G[B]
and
G[B^{\prime}]
are
quasi-threshold graphs by Theorem
3.4
.

(iv)

There is an edge between
v_{i}\in A
and

x\in B
if and only if
v_{i}\in N_{A}[H_{x}]
, and there is an edge
between
v^{\prime}_{i}\in A^{\prime}
and
x\in B^{\prime}
if and only if

v^{\prime}_{i}\in N_{A^{\prime}}[H^{\prime}_{x}]
.

By the fact that
G[A]
and
G[A^{\prime}]
are threshold graphs in even and odd template partitions, by Properties
(ii)
and
(iv)
of even template partitions (which are the same as Properties
(ii)
and
(iv)
of odd templates) and conditions (
-
‣
f
) and (
-
‣
g
) of even template partitions, we have the following lemma whose proof is similar to the one of Lemma
4.4
.

Lemma 8.1

There exist vertices
w
and
w^{\prime}
that are universal vertices in
respectively
G[A\cup B]
and
G[A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime}]
.

Lemma 8.2

For an integer
\ell\geq 4
, every theta
\Theta
such that
\Theta\in\mathcal{C}_{2\ell}
has an even
\ell
-template partition, every prism
\Sigma
such that
\Sigma\in\mathcal{C}_{2\ell}
has an even
\ell
-template partition.

Proof.

Since
\Theta\in\mathcal{C}_{2\ell}
, its three paths have
length
\ell
. Let
x
and
y
be the common two extremities of these paths, let
A
and
A^{\prime}
be respectively the set of neighbors of
x
and
y
and let
I
be the set of vertices of
\Theta
that are not in
A\cup A^{\prime}\cup\{x,y\}
. It is easy to verify that
(A,\{x\},A^{\prime},\{y\},I,x,y)
is an even
\ell
-template partition. Similarly all three paths of
\Sigma
have length
\ell-1
. Let
A
be the set of the vertices
v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}
of one of the triangles and
A^{\prime}
be the set of vertices
v^{\prime}_{1},v^{\prime}_{2},v^{\prime}_{3}
of the other triangle. It is easy to verify that
(A,\varnothing,A^{\prime},\varnothing,V(\Sigma)\setminus(A\cup A^{\prime}),v_{1},v^{\prime}_{1})
is an even
\ell
-template partition.
∎

Due to the similarities in odd and even template partitions as pointed out above we also get the following results similar to Lemmas
4.6
,
4.7
and
4.8

with the same proofs.

Lemma 8.3

If
x\in B
(resp.
x\in B^{\prime}
), then
H_{x}
(resp.
H^{\prime}_{x}
) is the
unique anticomponent of
G[N_{A}(x)]
(resp.
G[N_{A^{\prime}}(x)]
) that
contains at least two vertices.

Lemma 8.4

If
x,y\in B
(resp.
x,y\in B^{\prime}
) are such that
xy\notin E(G)
,
then
H_{x}\cup\{x\}
(resp.
H^{\prime}_{x}\cup\{x\}
) is anticomplete to

H_{y}\cup\{y\}
(resp.
H^{\prime}_{y}\cup\{y\}
).

Lemma 8.5

Every vertex of
G
has degree at least 2 and every vertex of

B\cup B^{\prime}
has degree at least 3.

We may also extend
\mathcal{H}
into a hypergraph
\mathcal{H}_{A}
with vertex-set
A
by adding to its edge-set the hyperedge
H_{v}=N_{A}[v]\cap\{u\in A:u\leq_{G[A]}v\}
for every vertex
v\in A
. Similarly we extend
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
into a hypergraph
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}}
. The following lemma has the same proof as Lemma
4.10
.

Lemma 8.6

\mathcal{H}_{A}
is a laminar hypergraph and
G[A\cup B]
is
isomorphic to its line graph (in particular,
G[A\cup B]
is a
quasi-threshold graph and therefore a chordal graph). A similar
statements holds for
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}}
and
G[A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime}]
.

8.2
Even
\ell
-templates

We will now need more notion and notation.

Given an even
\ell
-template partition of
G
, we define a hypergraph
\mathcal{H}_{G}
whose vertex set is
\{k+1,k+2,\ldots k+s\}
and whose hyperedges are sets of indices of the vertices of
A_{S}\cup A_{S^{\prime}}
in hyperedges of
\mathcal{H}_{A}\cup\mathcal{H}_{A^{\prime}}
.
More formally,
E(\mathcal{H}_{G})=E_{A}\cup E_{A^{\prime}}
where

-
E_{A}=\{\{i:v_{i}\in H\cap A_{S}\}:H\text{ hyperedge of }\mathcal{H}_{A}\text{ s.t. }H\cap A_{S}\neq\varnothing\}

-
E_{A^{\prime}}=\{\{i:v_{i}\in H\cap A^{\prime}_{S}\}:H\text{ hyperedge of }\mathcal{H}_{A^{\prime}}\text{ s.t. }H\cap A^{\prime}_{S}\neq\varnothing\}
.

Notice that
\mathcal{H}_{G}
may contain distinct hyperedges containing the same set of vertices.

A circular sequence
\mathcal{C}=(j_{1},e_{1},j_{2},...,j_{t},e_{t},j_{1})
, where the
j_{i}
’s are distinct vertices of
\mathcal{H}_{G}
and the
e_{i}
’s are distinct hyperedges of
\mathcal{H}_{G}
, is said to be a
hyper cycle
of length
t
of
\mathcal{H}_{G}
if

•

each
j_{i}
belongs to
e_{i-1}
and
e_{i}
(where
e_{t+1}=e_{1})
and to no other hyperedge of
\mathcal{C}
,

•

any two distinct hyperedges of
\mathcal{C}
that belong both to
E_{A}
or both to
E_{A^{\prime}}
are disjoint.

We notice that by definition each
e_{i}
contains
j_{i}
and
j_{i+1}
and no other vertex of
\mathcal{C}
, hence any two consecutive hyperedges
e_{i}
and
e_{i+1}
of
\mathcal{C}
have a non empty intersection and none is included in the other. So, since
\mathcal{H}_{A}
and
\mathcal{H}_{A^{\prime}}
are both laminar hypergraphs, the hyperedges of
\mathcal{C}
belong alternately to
E_{A}
and
E_{A^{\prime}}
. In particular the length of
\mathcal{C}
is even.

For an integer
\ell\geq 3
and a graph
G
, a
strong even
\ell
-template partition
of
G
is an even
\ell
-template partition
(A,A^{\prime},B,B^{\prime},I)
of
G
, such that
\mathcal{H}_{G}
contains no hyper cycle of length greater than
2
.

A graph
G
which has a strong even
\ell
-template partition is called an
even
\ell
-template
. We will denote by
(A,B,A^{\prime},B^{\prime},I,w,w^{\prime})
an even
\ell
-template
G
with strong even
\ell
-template partition
(A,B,A^{\prime},B^{\prime},I)
such that
w
is a universal vertex of
G[A\cup B]
and
w^{\prime}
is a universal vertex of
G[A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime}]
(by Lemma
8.1
, such
w
and
w^{\prime}
do exist).

Lemma 8.7

Let
G
be an even
\ell
-template and
(A,A^{\prime},B,B^{\prime},I)
be a strong even
\ell
-template partition of
G
.
Every hole
H
of
G
contains two principal paths of
G
and either

-

these two principal paths have length
\ell-1
and they induce
H
,

-

one principal path has length
\ell-1
, the other has length
\ell-2
and
H
contains exactly one more vertex which belongs to
A_{K}\cup B\cup A^{\prime}_{K}\cup B^{\prime}
,

-

both principal paths have length
\ell-2
,
H
contains exactly two more vertices, one in
A_{K}\cup B
and the other in
A^{\prime}_{K}\cup B^{\prime}
.

In all cases
H
has length
2\ell
, and so
G\in\mathcal{C}_{2\ell}
.

Proof.

Let
H
be a hole of
G
. By Lemma
8.6
,
G[A\cup B]
contains no
P_{4}
and no
C_{4}
, so
H
cannot contain only vertices of

A\cup B
, and similarly, it cannot contain only vertices of
A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime}
. So
H

must contain vertices of some principal path, and hence it contains an even number of
principal paths.

In case
H
contains two principal paths of length
\ell-1
then their endpoints are linked by two disjoint edges (by the definition of an even template partition). So these paths form a hole of length
2\ell
and
H
contains no other principal path.

Assume now that
H
contains exactly one path
P_{u}=u\ldots u^{\prime}
of length
\ell-1
and at least one path
P_{v}=v\ldots v^{\prime}
of length
\ell-2
, for some
u\in A_{K}
and
v\in A_{S}
. By the definition of an even template partition, there exists exactly one edge between
P_{u}
and any principal path of length
\ell-2
, hence
H
cannot contain three such paths and should hence contain exactly one, namely
P_{v}
. Up to symmetry, we may assume that
uv\notin E(G)
and then the hole
H
is
made of the path
uP_{u}u^{\prime}v^{\prime}P_{v}v
of
length
2\ell-2
and a path
Q=u\dots v
of
G[A\cup B]
. By
Lemma
8.6
,
Q
has length at most 2 and since
uv\notin E(G)
, we get that
Q=ubv
for some
b\in A\cup B
. So
H
has length
2\ell
and since
A_{S}
is a stable set in
G[A]
we have
b\in A_{K}\cup B
as claimed.

It remains to consider the case where all principal paths contained in
H
have length
\ell-2
. Assume that
H
contains
t\geq 3
principal paths
P_{1},P_{2},\ldots P_{t}
associated to some
v_{j_{1}},v_{j_{2}},\ldots v_{j_{t}}\in A_{S}
(note that since
t
is even, then
t\geq 4
). By the definition of an even template partition, there exists no edge connecting these paths. Since by Lemma
8.6
there is no
P_{4}
in
G[A\cup B]
and in
G[A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime}]
, without loss of generality we may assume that
H=v_{j_{1}}P_{1}v^{\prime}_{j_{1}}b_{1}v^{\prime}_{j_{2}}P_{2}v_{j_{2}}b_{2}v_{j_{3}},\ldots v^{\prime}_{j_{t}}P_{t}v_{j_{t}}b_{t}v_{j_{1}}
where
b_{i}
’s with odd index belong to
A^{\prime}_{K}\cup B^{\prime}
and those with even index belong to
A_{K}\cup B
.
Hence, to each
b_{i}
we may associate the hyperedge
e_{i}
of
\mathcal{H}_{G}
corresponding to
H_{b_{i}}\in\mathcal{H}_{A}\cup\mathcal{H}_{A^{\prime}}
. We claim that
\mathcal{C}=(j_{1},e_{1},j_{2},...,j_{t},e_{t},j_{1})
is a hyper cycle of
\mathcal{H}_{G}
. Assume there exists in
\mathcal{C}
an hyperedge
e_{l}\neq e_{i-1},e_{i}
such that
j_{i}\in e_{l}
. So, up to symmetry,
v_{j_{i}}\in H_{b_{l}}
and then by the definition of an even template,
H
would contain a chord
v_{j_{i}}b_{l}
, a contradiction. So the first condition for being a hyper cycle is satisfied by
\mathcal{C}
. Assume now, up to symmetry, that there exist
e_{i},e_{l}\in H_{A}
that are not disjoint. Then by the definition of an even template partition,
H
would contain a chord
b_{i}b_{l}
, a contradiction again. Hence the second condition should be satisfied and
\mathcal{C}
is a hyper cycle of
\mathcal{H}_{G}
, a contradiction to the fact that
(A,A^{\prime},B,B^{\prime},I)
is a strong even
\ell
-template partition of a graph
G
.
So, we may now conclude that
H
contains exactly two principal paths of length
\ell-2
and two more vertices
b_{1}\in A_{K}\cup B
and
b_{2}\in A^{\prime}_{K}\cup B^{\prime}
. ∎

8.3
Even
\ell
-pretemplates

For every integer
\ell\geq 4
, an
even
\ell
-pretemplate partition
of a
graph
G
is a partition of the vertex-set of
G
into five sets
A=A_{K}\cup A_{S}
,
B
,

A^{\prime}=A^{\prime}_{K}\cup A^{\prime}_{S}
,
B^{\prime}
and
I
that satisfy the following conditions.

1.

N(B)\subseteq A
and
N(A\cup B)\subseteq I
.

2.

N(B^{\prime})\subseteq A^{\prime}
and
N(A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime})\subseteq I
.

3.

|A_{K}|=|A^{\prime}_{K}|=k
,
A_{K}=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}
and

A^{\prime}_{K}=\{v^{\prime}_{1},\dots,v^{\prime}_{k}\}
.

4.

|A_{S}|=|A^{\prime}_{S}|=s
,
A_{S}=\{v_{k+1},\dots,v_{k+s}\}
and

A^{\prime}_{S}=\{v^{\prime}_{k+1},\dots,v^{\prime}_{k+s}\}
are stable sets of
G
where
k+s\geq 3
.

5.

For every
i\in\{1,\dots,k+s\}
, there exists a
unique path
P_{i}
from
v_{i}
to
v^{\prime}_{i}
whose interior is in
I
.

6.

Every vertex in
I
has degree 2 and lies on a path from
v_{i}

to
v^{\prime}_{i}
for some
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
.

7.

All paths
P_{1}
, …,
P_{k}
have length
\ell-1
, all paths
P_{k+1}
, …,
P_{k+s}
have length
\ell-2
.

8.

G[A\cup B]
and
G[A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime}]
are both connected
graphs.

9.

Every vertex of
B
is in the interior of a path of

G[A\cup B]
with both ends in
A
.

10.

Every vertex of
B^{\prime}
is in the interior of a path of

G[A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime}]
with both ends in
A^{\prime}
.

We then say that
(A,A^{\prime},B,B^{\prime},I)
is an even
\ell
-pretemplate
partition
of
G
.

Lemma 8.8

Let
\ell\geq 4
be an integer and
G
be a graph of
\mathcal{C}_{2\ell}
. Any even
\ell
-pretemplate partition
(A,B,A^{\prime},B^{\prime},I)
of
G
is a strong even
\ell
-template partition of
G
.

Proof.

(
53)
For all distinct
i,j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
,
v_{i}v_{j}\in E(G)
and

v^{\prime}_{i}v^{\prime}_{j}\in E(G)
. In particular,
A_{K}
and
A^{\prime}_{K}
are cliques of
G
.

Assume that at least one of
v_{i}v_{j},v^{\prime}_{i}v^{\prime}_{j}\notin E(G)
. By condition
8.
in the definition of an even pretemplate partition
there exist a path between
v_{i}
and
v_{j}
in
G[A\cup B]
and a
path between
v^{\prime}_{i}
and
v^{\prime}_{j}
in
G[A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime}]
. Together with
P_{i}
and
P_{j}
, these paths form a hole of length at
least
2\ell+1
, a contradiction.This proves (53).

(
54)
For all distinct
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
and
j\in\{k+1,\dots,k+s\}
, exactly one of
v_{i}v_{j}
and

v^{\prime}_{i}v^{\prime}_{j}
is an edge of
G
.

By condition
8.
in the definition of an even pretemplate partition
there exist a path between
v_{i}
and
v_{j}
in
G[A\cup B]
and a
path between
v^{\prime}_{i}
and
v^{\prime}_{j}
in
G[A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime}]
. Together with the principal paths
P_{i}
(of length
\ell-1)
and
P_{j}
(of length
\ell-2)
, these paths form a hole which will be of length
2\ell
if and only if one of the paths is of length
1
and the other path is of length
2
.
This proves (54).

(
55)
Every path of
G[A\cup B]
with both ends in
A
is of length at
most 2. The same holds for a path of
G[A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime}]
with both ends in
A^{\prime}
.

Assume on the contrary that there exists a path
P
of length at least
3
in
G[A\cup B]
with both ends
v_{i},v_{j}
in

A
. Then by (
8.3
) and condition
7.
of an even pretemplate partition, at least one of
P_{i},P_{j}
is of length
\ell-2
, say
P_{j}
. If
P_{i}
is of length
\ell-1
then by (
8.3
)
v^{\prime}_{i}v^{\prime}_{j}\in E(G)
and
P
,
P_{i}
,
P_{j}
would induce a hole of length at least
2\ell+1
, a contradiction. Hence
P_{i}
and
P_{j}
are both of length
\ell-2
and by conditions
4.
and
8.
of an even pretemplate partition,
P
,
P_{i}
,
P_{j}
and any
path between
v^{\prime}_{i}
and
v^{\prime}_{j}
form a hole of length at least
2\ell+1
, a
contradiction again.
The proof for
G[A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime}]
is similar.
This proves (55).

(
56)

G[A]
is a threshold graph. The same holds for
G[A^{\prime}]
.

G[A]
is obviously
C_{4}
-free. By (
8.3
) and condition
4.
of an even pretemplate partition,
A
is partitioned into a clique and a stable set of
G
, so by Theorem
3.3

G[A]
is
2K_{2}
-free. By (
8.3
),

G[A]
is
P_{4}
-free. So
G[A]
is (
P_{4}
,
C_{4}
,
2K_{2}
)-free and
is therefore a threshold graph. The proof for
G[A^{\prime}]
is similar.
This proves (56).

We now study the structure of
G[B]
(respectively
G[B^{\prime}]
) and its relation with
G[A]
(respectively
G[A^{\prime}]
).

(
57)
For every vertex
x\in B
,
G[N_{A}(x)]
has a unique anticonnected
component of size at least 2. The same holds for
G[N_{A^{\prime}}(x)]
when
x
is any vertex in
B^{\prime}
.

By condition
9.
of an even pretemplate partition,
x
is in the interior of a path

P=v_{i}\dots v_{j}
of
G[A\cup B]
with both ends in
A
.
By (
8.3
),
P
has length 2, so
x
is adjacent to
v_{i}

and
v_{j}
. Hence
G[N_{A}(x)]
has an anticonnected component of size
at least 2. It is unique, for otherwise
G[A]
would contain a
C_{4}
.
This proves (57).

For all
x\in B
(respectively
B^{\prime}
), we define
H_{x}
to be the anticonnected component
of
G[N_{A}(x)]
(respectively
G[N_{A}^{\prime}(x)]
) of size at least 2 whose existence follows
from (
8.3
).

(
58)
For every
x
in
B
(respectively
B^{\prime}
),
H_{x}
is a module of
G[A]
(respectively
G[A^{\prime}]
).

Let
x\in B
. If the claim does not hold, since
H_{x}
is by definition anticonnected, there
exist
v_{h}\in A\setminus H_{x}
and non-adjacent
v_{i},v_{j}\in H_{x}
such
that
v_{h}v_{i}\in E(G)
and
v_{h}v_{j}\notin E(G)
. Note that

xv_{h}\notin E(G)
because otherwise,
v_{h}
would be in
H_{x}
. Then
v_{h}v_{i}xv_{j}
is a path of length
3
, a contradiction to (
8.3
). The similar proof holds for
x
in
B^{\prime}
.
This proves (58).

(
59)
If
xy
is an edge of
G[B]
or
G[B^{\prime}]
, then
H_{x}\subseteq H_{y}
or

H_{y}\subseteq H_{x}
.

Let
xy
be an edge of
G[B].
Up to symmetry, we may assume that
N_{A}(x)\subseteq N_{A}(y)
, for
otherwise vertices
v_{i}\in N_{A}(x)\setminus N_{A}(y)
and
v_{j}\in N_{A}(y)\setminus N_{A}(x)
either form a
C_{4}
with
x
and
y
or a contradiction to (
8.3
).

By (
8.3
),
G[N_{A}(y)]
has only one anticonnected component of size
at least 2, namely
H_{y}
. Since
H_{x}
is anticonnected, has size
at least 2 and is included in
N_{A}(y)
, it must be included in

H_{y}
. The similar proof holds for an edge
xy
of
G[B^{\prime}].

This proves (59).

(
60)
If
x
and
y
are non-adjacent vertices of
B
or
B^{\prime}
then
H_{x}
and

H_{y}
are disjoint.

On the contrary, suppose that
x
and
y
are nonadjacent vertices of
B
but
there exists a vertex
v\in H_{x}\cap H_{y}
. Since
H_{x}
is anticonnected and
of size at least 2, there exists
v_{i}\in H_{x}
non-adjacent to
v
. Note that

v_{i}y\notin E(G)
, for otherwise
x
,
y
,
v_{i}
and
v
form a
C_{4}
.
Similarly, there exists a vertex
v_{j}\in H_{y}
that is non-adjacent to
v
and
to
x
. If
v_{i}v_{j}\in E(G)
, then
\{x,y,v,v_{i},v_{j}\}
induces a
C_{5}
, a
contradiction. Otherwise,
\{x,y,v,v_{i},v_{j}\}
induces a
P_{5}
, a
contradiction to (
8.3
). The proof for
x,y\in B^{\prime}
is similar.
This proves (60).

We are now ready to define the hypergraphs
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
. For every

x\in B
(respectively
B^{\prime}
), we defined a set
H_{x}\subseteq A
(respectively
H_{x}\subseteq A^{\prime}
). From (
8.3
)
and (
8.3
), the sets
H_{x}
for
x\in B
form a laminar
hypergraph
\mathcal{H}
(with vertex set
A
). Symetrically, the
sets
H_{x}
for
x\in B^{\prime}
form a laminar hypergraph
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}

(with vertex set
A^{\prime}
).

(
61)
If
G[A]
is not connected then at least one hyperedge of
\mathcal{H}
contains all vertices of
A
.

Assume
G[A]
is not connected. By (
8.3
),
G[A]
is a threshold graph, and then by Theorem
3.7
it contains an isolated vertex
v_{i}
. By the definition of an even pretemplate partition,
G[A\cup B]
is connected and
|A|\geq 3
, so there exists a path
P
in

G[A\cup B]
from
v_{i}
to a vertex
u\in A\setminus\{v_{i}\}
. By (
8.3
) and since
v_{i}
has no neighbor in
A
, we have that
P=uyv_{i}
where
y\in B
. So,
H_{y}
contains
v_{i}
. We may
therefore consider the hyperedge
W
of
\mathcal{H}
that contains
v_{i}

and that is inclusion-wise maximal w.r.t. this property.
If there exists
v_{j}\in A\setminus W
, since

v_{j}v_{i}\notin E(G)
, we deduce as above that
\mathcal{H}
has a
hyperedge
Z
that contains
i
and
j
. Because of
v_{j}
,

Z\subseteq W
is impossible; because of
v_{i}
,
W\cap Z=\varnothing
is
impossible; and because of the maximality of
W
,
W\subsetneq Z
is
impossible. Hence,
W
and
Z
contradict
\mathcal{H}
being
laminar. So
W=A
.

This proves (61).

At this point we can easily verify that
(A,B,A^{\prime},B^{\prime},I)
is an even
\ell
-template partition of
G
:

- conditions (
a
) (
b
) (
c
) and (
d
) of an even template partition are satisfied because of conditions
3.

4.

5.

6.
and
7.
in the definition of an even pretemplate partition,

- (
e
) of an even template partition is a consequence of condition
4.
of an even pretemplate partition and (
8.3
), (
8.3
), (
8.3
),

- (
f
), (
g
) and (
h
) of even template partition is a consequence of (
8.3
), (
8.3
), (
8.3
), (
8.3
),

- (
i
) and (
j
) of an even template partition comes from (
8.3
) and (
8.3
),

- (
k
) and (
l
) of an even template partition follows easily from our previous results :
By definition of
H_{x}
, for every
x
in
B
,

N_{A}(x)\subseteq N_{A}[H_{x}]
. Suppose that there exists

u\in N_{A}[H_{x}]\setminus N_{A}(x)
. Since by (
8.3
)
H_{x}
is a module,
it follows from Lemma
4.2
that
u
is complete to
H_{x}
, so

x
and
u
together with two non-adjacent vertices from
H_{x}
induce
a
C_{4}
, a contradiction. Hence,
N_{A}(x)=N_{A}[H_{x}]
and
condition (
k
) of an even template partition is satisfied. The proof for (
l
) is similar.

It remains to prove the following.

(
62)
\mathcal{H}_{G}
contains no hyper cycle of length greater than
2
.

Suppose on the contrary that
\mathcal{C}=(j_{1},e_{1},j_{2},...,j_{t},e_{t},j_{1})
is a hyper cycle of
\mathcal{H}_{G}
of length
t>2
. Without loss of generality, each
e_{i}
with odd index belongs to
E_{A}
, and since it contains
j_{i}
and
j_{i+1}
such that
v_{j_{i}},v_{j_{i+1}}\in A_{S}
we have that
e_{i}
is the set of indices of the vertices of
H_{b_{i}}
for some
b_{i}\in A_{K}\cup B
. Similarly each
e_{i}
with even index belongs to
E_{A^{\prime}}
, contains
j_{i}
and
j_{i+1}
such that
v^{\prime}_{j_{i}}
and
v^{\prime}_{j_{i+1}}\in A^{\prime}_{S}
and corresponds to
